where on the right we have the inverse limit of the inverse system

\cdots\twoheadrightarrow OS\!ym_{1}\twoheadrightarrow OS\!ym_{0}

taken in the category of graded superalgebras,
with the map
OS\!ym_{n+1}\twoheadrightarrow OS\!ym_{n}
taking
e_{r}^{(n+1)}
to
e_{r}^{(n)}
. Moreover,
OS\!ym_{n}
may be identified with the quotient

OS\!ym\>\big/\>\langle e_{r}\>|\>r>n\rangle.

Corollary 4.7

(
[
EKL
, (2.20)]
)
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}OS\!ym_{n}=\prod_{r=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1-(\pi q^{2})^{r}}
.

Proof.

theorem

4.5
shows that
OS\!ym_{n}
has the same graded
superdimension as a commutative polynomial algebra with generators
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
such that
x_{r}
is of degree
2r
and parity
r\pmod{2}
.
∎

Corollary 4.8
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}=\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}OS\!ym_{n}\times q^{\binom{n}{2}}[n]^{!}_{q,\pi}=\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}OS\!ym_{n}\times\sum_{w\in S\!_{n}}(\pi q^{2})^{\ell(w)}
.

Proof.

The second equality follows from the first by
eq.

3.3
.
To obtain the first equality, we use
corollary

4.7
to see that

\displaystyle\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}OS\!ym_{n}\times q^{\binom{n}{2}}[n]^{!}_{q,\pi}=

\displaystyle q^{\binom{n}{2}}[n]^{!}_{q,\pi}\prod_{r=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1-(\pi q^{2})^{r}}=\;\frac{q^{\binom{n}{2}}[n]^{!}_{q,\pi}}{(1-\pi q^{2})^{n}}\prod_{r=1}^{n}\frac{1-\pi q^{2}}{1-(\pi q^{2})^{r}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\frac{[n]^{!}_{q,\pi}}{(1-\pi q^{2})^{n}}\prod_{r=1}^{n}\frac{\pi q-q^{-1}}{(\pi q)^{r}-q^{-r}}=\;\frac{1}{(1-\pi q^{2})^{n}}\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}.

∎

The next technical
lemma about relations in
OS\!ym_{n+1}

will be needed at a key place later on; see
lemma

11.2

which is used to prove
theorem

11.3
.

Lemma 4.9
.

For any
0\leq p,q,k\leq n
with
p+q\leq n
, we have that

\sum_{m=1}^{n-p-q}(-1)^{(n+k)(m+p+k)}e^{(n+1)}_{n-k+m}e^{(n+1)}_{n-p-q-m}=\sum_{m=1}^{n-p-q}(-1)^{(n+k)(m+q)}e^{(n+1)}_{n-p-q-m}e^{(n+1)}_{n-k+m}

in
OS\!ym_{n+1}
.

Proof.

If
p+q+k
is even then
(-1)^{(n+k)(m+p+k)}=(-1)^{(n+k)(m+q)}
,
and

e^{(n+1)}_{n-k+m}
commutes with
e^{(n+1)}_{n-p-q-m}

by the relation
eq.

4.4
.
The result obviously follows in this situation
since corresponding terms on each side are equal.

Next, assume that
p+q+k
is odd and
n\equiv p+q\pmod{2}
,
in which case
n+k
is odd.
There are an even number of terms in the summations in the identity we are trying to prove. It suffices to show that sums of consecutive pairs of terms on each side are equal, i.e.,

(-1)^{(n+k)(m+p+k)}e^{(n+1)}_{n-k+m}e^{(n+1)}_{n-p-q-m}+(-1)^{(n+k)(m+1+p+k)}e^{(n+1)}_{n-k+m+1}e^{(n+1)}_{n-p-q-m-1}=\\
(-1)^{(n+k)(m+q)}e^{(n+1)}_{n-p-q-m}e^{(n+1)}_{n-k+m}+(-1)^{(n+k)(m+1+q)}e^{(n+1)}_{n-p-q-m-1}e^{(n+1)}_{n-k+m+1}

for every odd
m
with
1\leq m<n-p-q
.
Multiplying both sides by
(-1)^{(n+k)(m+p+k)}=(-1)^{(n+k)(m+q+1)}
, this simplifies to

e^{(n+1)}_{n-k+m}e^{(n+1)}_{n-p-q-m}-e^{(n+1)}_{n-k+m+1}e^{(n+1)}_{n-p-q-m-1}=-e^{(n+1)}_{n-p-q-m}e^{(n+1)}_{n-k+m}+e^{(n+1)}_{n-p-q-m-1}e^{(n+1)}_{n-k+m+1}.

This follows because
when
m
is odd,
n-k+m
is even, so we have that

e_{n-k+m}e_{n-p-q-m}+e_{n-p-q-m}e_{n-k+m}=e_{n-k+m+1}e_{n-p-q-m-1}+e_{n-p-q-m-1}e_{n-k+m+1}

by
eq.

4.5
.

Finally we treat the case that
p+q+k
is odd and
n\not\equiv p+q\pmod{2}
,
when
n+k
is even. Now the signs on both sides of
the identity we are trying to prove
are always
+
so can be omitted.
There are an odd number of terms in the summations. The
m=n-p-q
terms in both
summations
are equal, indeed, they both equal

e^{(n+1)}_{2n-p-q-k}
as
e^{(n+1)}_{0}=1
.
To see that the remaining terms in the summations are equal, we
show that sums of consecutive pairs of terms are equal like in the previous paragraph, i.e.,

e^{(n+1)}_{n-k+m}e^{(n+1)}_{n-p-q-m}+e^{(n+1)}_{n-k+m+1}e^{(n+1)}_{n-p-q-m-1}=e^{(n+1)}_{n-p-q-m}e^{(n+1)}_{n-k+m}+e^{(n+1)}_{n-p-q-m-1}e^{(n+1)}_{n-k+m+1}

for each odd
m
with
1\leq m<n-p-q-1
.
This follows because when
n-k+m
is odd we have that

e_{n-k+m}e_{n-p-q-m}-e_{n-p-q-m}e_{n-k+m}=-e_{n-k+m+1}e_{n-p-q-m-1}+e_{n-p-q-m-1}e_{n-k+m+1}

by
eq.

4.5
again.
∎

It is time to say a little more about
variants of the odd complete and elementary symmetric functions. The following lemma, which is another application of
theorem

4.4
, is helpful to understand the possibilities.

Lemma 4.10
.

We have that

{\gamma}(h_{r})=(-1)^{\binom{r}{2}}h_{r}^{*}
and

{\gamma}(e_{r})=(-1)^{\binom{r}{2}}e_{r}^{*}

for any
r\geq 0
.
Hence,
*\circ{\gamma}\circ\psi=\psi\circ*\circ{\gamma}
.

Proof.

It is immediate from the definitions that
{\gamma}(e_{r})=(-1)^{\binom{r}{2}}e_{r}^{*}
.
To see the analogous thing for
h_{r}
,
it suffices to show that

{\gamma}_{n}\big(h^{(n)}_{r}\big)^{*}=(-1)^{\binom{r}{2}}h_{r}^{(n)}

for all
r\geq 0
.
This follows from
the explicit descriptions of these polynomials and maps given in

theorem

4.4
.
To deduce finally that

*\circ{\gamma}
and
\psi
commute, it suffices to check that

(*\circ{\gamma}\circ\psi)(e_{r})=(\psi\circ*\circ{\gamma})(e_{r})

for all
r\geq 0
, which is clear at this point.
∎

As we have said before, our odd complete symmetric function

h_{r}
is the same as the
h_{r}

in
[
EK
,
EKL
]
, but our odd elementary symmetric function
e_{r}

is different from the one there, which is

(4.48)

\varepsilon_{r}:={\gamma}(e_{r})=(-1)^{\binom{r}{2}}e_{r}^{*}=(-1)^{\binom{r}{2}}e_{r},

where the non-trivial equality follows by
lemma

4.10
.
There is also a natural variant on the odd complete symmetric function
h_{r}
, namely,

(4.49)

\eta_{r}:={\gamma}(h_{r})=(-1)^{\binom{r}{2}}h_{r}^{*}.

Since
h_{r}^{*}\neq h_{r}

for
r>1
, it is
not
the case that
\eta_{r}=(-1)^{\binom{r}{2}}h_{r}
.
We call
\varepsilon_{r}
and
\eta_{r}
the

dual odd elementary and complete symmetric functions
.
Applying
{\gamma}
to
eq.

4.10
gives that

(4.50)

\displaystyle\varepsilon(t)\eta(t)

\displaystyle=\eta(t)\varepsilon(t)=1.

where
\varepsilon(t):=\sum_{r\geq 0}(-1)^{r}\varepsilon_{r}t^{-r}
and

\eta(t):=\sum_{r\geq 0}\eta_{r}t^{-r}
.
These should make it clear that

e
and
h
belong together as do
\varepsilon
and
\eta
. It is
not so easy to relate
e
to
\eta
or
h
to
\varepsilon
in terms of
generating functions; cf.
eq.

10.29
.

Consider again the truncation
OS\!ym_{n}
.
Let
\varepsilon_{r}^{(n)}
and
\eta_{r}^{(n)}

be the images
\varepsilon_{r}
and
\eta_{r}

in
OS\!ym_{n}
.
From
theorem

4.4
, it is clear that

(4.51)

\displaystyle e_{r}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{r}\leq n}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{r}}

\displaystyle h^{(n)}_{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq i_{r}\geq\cdots\geq i_{1}\geq 1}x_{i_{r}}\cdots x_{i_{1}}

(4.52)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{r}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq i_{r}>\dots>i_{1}\geq 1}x_{i_{r}}\cdots x_{i_{1}},

\displaystyle\eta^{(n)}_{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{r}\leq n}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{r}}.

This gives an explanation for the existence of the four basic familes of
odd symmetric functions
e_{r},h_{r},\varepsilon_{r}
and

\eta_{r}
.
When working in
OS\!ym_{n}
with generating functions, we prefer to modify
the definitions slightly, working not exactly with the images
of
e(t),h(t),\varepsilon(t)
and
\gamma(t)

in
OS\!ym_{n}\llbracket t^{-1}\rrbracket
, but incorporating a shift in
t
:

(4.53)

\displaystyle e^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{r=0}^{n}(-1)^{r}e^{(n)}_{r}t^{n-r},

\displaystyle h^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{r\geq 0}h^{(n)}_{r}t^{-n-r},

(4.54)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{r=0}^{n}(-1)^{r}\varepsilon^{(n)}_{r}t^{n-r},

\displaystyle\eta^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{r\geq 0}\eta^{(n)}_{r}t^{-n-r}.

The advantage of this is that
e^{(n)}(t)

and
\varepsilon^{(n)}(t)
are
polynomials

OS\!ym_{n}[t]
,
indeed, we have that

(4.55)

\displaystyle e^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle=(t-x_{1})\cdots(t-x_{n}),

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle=(t-x_{n})\cdots(t-x_{1}).

Also,
noting that
(t-x)^{-1}=t^{-1}+xt^{-2}+x^{2}t^{-3}+\cdots\in\mathbb{F}[x]\llbracket t^{-1}\rrbracket
,
we have that

(4.56)

\displaystyle h^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle=(t-x_{n})^{-1}\cdots(t-x_{1})^{-1},

\displaystyle\eta^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle=(t-x_{1})^{-1}\cdots(t-x_{n})^{-1}

in
OS\!ym_{n}\llbracket t^{-1}\rrbracket
.
Now we have that

(4.57)

\displaystyle h^{(n)}(t)e^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle=e^{(n)}(t)h^{(n)}(t)=1,

\displaystyle\eta^{(n)}(t)\varepsilon^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{(n)}(t)\eta^{(n)}(t)=1,

equality in the ring

OS\!ym_{n}(\!(t^{-1})\!)

of formal Laurent series in
t^{-1}
.

The next result is elementary but does not appear in the existing literature. Observe by
eq.

4.3
that

(4.58)

z_{2r}:=\sum_{s=0}^{r}e_{2s}h_{2r-2s}=\delta_{r,0}+\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}e_{2s+1}h_{2r-2s-1}.

The element
z_{2r}
is
central
: it commutes with all even
e_{t}
by the first form of the definition, and it commutes with all odd
e_{t}
by the second one. Also let
omicron be the special element

(4.59)

o:=e_{1}=h_{1},

noting that
z_{2}=o^{2}
. The relations
eqs.

4.5
and
4.2
imply that

(4.60)

\displaystyle e_{2r+1}

\displaystyle=\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\big(oe_{2r}+e_{2r}o\big),

\displaystyle h_{2r+1}

\displaystyle=\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\big(oh_{2r}+h_{2r}o\big),

so that
OS\!ym
is generated already by
o
and all even
e_{2r}\>(r\geq 1)
.

Theorem 4.11
.

The graded superalgebra
OS\!ym
is generated by
o
and
e_{2r}\>(r\geq 1)
subject only to the relations

(4.61)

\displaystyle[e_{2r},e_{2s}]

\displaystyle=0

(4.62)

\displaystyle[o^{2},e_{2r}]

\displaystyle=0

(4.63)

\displaystyle[o,e_{2r+2}]

\displaystyle=\textstyle\left[\frac{1}{2}\big(oe_{2r}+e_{2r}o\big),e_{2}\right]

for
r,s\geq 1
.

Proof.

Let
A
be the graded superalgebra generated by an odd element
o

of degree 2 and even elements

e_{2r}\>(r\geq 1)
of degree
4r
subject to the relations
eqs.

4.61
,
4.62
and
4.63
.
For
r\geq 0
, we set
e_{1}:=o
and
e_{2r+1}:=\frac{1}{2}\big(oe_{2r}+e_{2r}o\big)\in A
for
r\geq 1
; cf.
eq.

4.60
.

We first construct a homomorphism
\alpha:A\rightarrow OS\!ym
by mapping
o\mapsto o
and
e_{r}\mapsto e_{r}
. To see that this makes sense, we need to check that the relations
eqs.

4.61
,
4.62
and
4.63
hold in
OS\!ym
. The first is immediate, and the second follows because we have observed already
that
z_{2}=o^{2}
is central in
OS\!ym
. For the third, in
OS\!ym
, we have that
[o,e_{2r+2}]=[e_{2r+1},e_{2}]
by
eq.

4.5
, and
also
\frac{1}{2}(oe_{2r}+e_{2r}o)=e_{2r+1}
by
eq.

4.60
. Now the relation is clear.

Next we construct a homomorphism
\beta:OS\!ym\rightarrow A
in the other direction so that on generators it sends
e_{r}\mapsto e_{r}
for each
r\geq 1
. To show this is well defined, we must again check relations, this time showing that
eqs.

4.4
and
4.5
hold in
A
. The first one is immediate if
r
and
s
are both even.
When
r
is odd and
s
is even,
eq.

4.5
is equivalent to the relation

(4.64)

[e_{2r-1},e_{2s}]=[e_{2s-1},e_{2r}]

for
r,s\geq 1
. To check it holds in
A
, we must show that the expression
[e_{2r-1},e_{2s}]\in A
is symmetric in
r
and
s
. We have that

\displaystyle 4[e_{2r-1},e_{2s}]

\displaystyle=2[oe_{2r-2}+e_{2r-2}o,e_{2s}]

\displaystyle=2oe_{2r-2}e_{2s}+2e_{2r-2}oe_{2s}-2e_{2s}oe_{2r-2}-2e_{2s}e_{2r-2}o

\displaystyle=2(oe_{2s}-e_{2s}o)e_{2r-2}+2e_{2r-2}(oe_{2s}-e_{2s}o)

\displaystyle=2[o,e_{2s}]e_{2r-2}+2e_{2r-2}[o,e_{2s}]

\displaystyle=[oe_{2s-2}+e_{2s-2}o,e_{2}]e_{2r-2}+e_{2r-2}[oe_{2s-2}+e_{2s-2}o,e_{2}]

\displaystyle=[o,e_{2}]e_{2s-2}e_{2r-2}+e_{2s-2}[o,e_{2}]e_{2r-2}+e_{2r-2}[o,e_{2}]e_{2s-2}+e_{2r-2}e_{2s-2}[o,e_{2}],

which is indeed symmetric in
r
and
s
. When
r
is even and
s
is odd,
eq.

4.5
is equivalent to the relation

(4.65)

[e_{2r},e_{2s+1}]_{+}=[e_{2s},e_{2r+1}]_{+}

for
r,s\geq 0
, where
[x,y]_{+}
here denotes
xy+yx
. So again we must show that
[e_{2r},e_{2s+1}]_{+}\in A
is symmetric in
r
and
s
. We have that

2[e_{2r},e_{2s+1}]_{+}=e_{2r}oe_{2s}+e_{2r}e_{2s}o+oe_{2s}e_{2r}+e_{2s}oe_{2r}.

This is symmetric in
r
and
s
because
e_{2r}e_{2s}=e_{2s}e_{2r}
. It remains to check the relation
eq.

4.4
when
r
and
s
are both odd. Equivalently, we show that
[e_{2r+1},e_{2s+1}]=0
for
r,s\geq 0
by induction on
s
. The base case
s=0
follows because

2[e_{2r+1},o]=[oe_{2r}+e_{2r}o,o]=oe_{2r}o+e_{2r}o^{2}-o^{2}e_{2r}-oe_{2r}o=-\left[o^{2},e_{2r}\right]=0.

The following establishes the induction step: for
s>0
we have in
A
that

\displaystyle 2[e_{2r+1},e_{2s+1}]

\displaystyle=[e_{2r+1},oe_{2s}+e_{2s}o]=o[e_{2r+1},e_{2s}]+[e_{2r+1},e_{2s}]o

\displaystyle=o[e_{2s-1},e_{2r+2}]+[e_{2s-1},e_{2r+2}]o=[e_{2s-1},oe_{2r+2}+e_{2r+2}o]

\displaystyle=2[e_{2s-1},e_{2r+3}]=-2[e_{2r+3},e_{2s-1}]=0,

using the
s=0
case for the second and fourth equalities,
eq.

4.64
for the third equality, and the induction hypothesis for the final equality.

It remains to observe that
\alpha
and
\beta
are two-sided inverses. This is obviously the case on generators by the way we have defined the maps.
∎

In the corollaries, we use the following notation:

•

S\!ym
is the algebra of symmetric
functions over
\mathbb{F}
as in

[
Mac
]
viewed as a graded superalgebra so that the
r
th elementary and complete symmetric functions are even of degree
4r
;

•

S\!ym_{n}
is the usual algebra of symmetric
polynomials in
n
variables, i.e., it is the quotient of
S\!ym
obtained by setting the
r
th even elementary symmetric polynomials
to zero for all
r>n
;

•

S\!ym[c]
and
S\!ym_{n}[c]
are the supercommutative graded superalgebras
obtained from
S\!ym
and
S\!ym_{n}

by adjoining an odd element
c
of degree 2
with
c^{2}=0
.

Corollary 4.12
.

The largest supercommutative quotient
of
OS\!ym
is the graded superalgebra

(4.66)

R:=OS\!ym\big/\big\langle o^{2},[o,e_{2}]\big\rangle.

Writing
\dot{a}
for the canonical image of
a\in OS\!ym

in
R
,
we have that

(4.67)

\displaystyle\dot{e}_{2r+1}

\displaystyle=\dot{e}_{2r}\dot{o},

\displaystyle\dot{h}_{2r+1}

\displaystyle=\dot{h}_{2r}\dot{o},

\displaystyle\dot{\varepsilon}_{2r+1}

\displaystyle=\dot{\varepsilon}_{2r}\dot{o},

\displaystyle\dot{\eta}_{2r+1}

\displaystyle=\dot{\eta}_{2r}\dot{o},

\displaystyle\dot{z}_{2r}

\displaystyle=\delta_{r,0}

for all
r\geq 0
.
Moreover,
there is an isomorphism of graded superalgebras

\theta:R\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits}}S\!ym[c]

taking
\dot{o}
to
c
,
\dot{\varepsilon}_{2r}=(-1)^{r}\dot{e}_{2r}

to the

r
th even elementary symmetric function and
\dot{h}_{2r}=(-1)^{r}\dot{\eta}_{2r}
to the

r
th even complete symmetric function.

Proof.

In any supercommutative quotient of
OS\!ym
, we must have that
o^{2}=0
and
[o,e_{2}]=0
. Now we let
I
be the two-sided ideal of
OS\!ym
generated by
o^{2}
and
[o,e_{2}]
and show that
OS\!ym/I
is supercommutative.
Since
OS\!ym
is generated by the elements

e_{2r}\>(r\geq 1)
and
o
, the proof of this reduces at once to checking that

[o,e_{2r}]\in I
for all
r\geq 1
, which holds because

2[o,e_{2r}]=2[e_{2r-1},e_{2}]=[e_{2r-2}o+oe_{2r-2},e_{2}]=e_{2r-2}[o,e_{2}]+[o,e_{2}]e_{2r-2}\in I.

Thus, we have shown that
R:=OS\!ym/I
is the largest supercommutative quotient of
OS\!ym
.
Next we observe that

\dot{e}_{2r+1}=\dot{e}_{2r}\dot{o}
,

\dot{h}_{2r+1}=\dot{h}_{2r}\dot{o}

and
\dot{z}_{2r}=\delta_{r,0}
for all
r\geq 0
.
The first two of these follow from
eq.

4.60
and the supercommutativity of
OS\!ym/I
,
then the final equality follows using the first two together with the second form of the definition of
z_{2r}
in
eq.

4.58
.
The superalgebra
anti-involution
*:OS\!ym\rightarrow OS\!ym
induces an anti-involution of

R
. Since it fixes the generators

\dot{e}_{r}\>(r\geq 1)

and
R
is supercommutative, this induced anti-involution is
actually the identity.
So by
eqs.

4.48
and
4.49
,
we have that

\dot{\varepsilon}_{r}=(-1)^{\binom{r}{2}}\dot{e}_{r}

and
\dot{\eta}_{r}=(-1)^{\binom{r}{2}}\dot{h}_{r}
.
The remaining identities in
eq.

4.67
follow
using this.

Finally, we construct the isomorphism
\theta
.
We start by observing that there is a homomorphism

OS\!ym\rightarrow S\!ym[c]
taking
o\mapsto c
and
\varepsilon_{2r}=(-1)^{r}e_{2r}
to the
r
th even
elementary symmetric function for
r\geq 1
.
To see this, we apply
theorem

4.11
to reduce to checking that the relations
eqs.

4.61
,
4.62
and
4.63
all hold in
S\!ym[c]
, which is clear because it is supercommutative.
Now this homomorphism factors through the quotient to induce
\theta:R\rightarrow S\!ym[c]
.
Moreover,
R
is spanned by the monomials
\dot{e}_{\lambda}
and
\dot{e}_{\lambda}\dot{o}
for partitions
\lambda
with all parts even. The images under
\theta
of these elements give a linear basis for
S\!ym[c]
. This shows that
\theta
is an isomorphism.
It just remains to check that
\theta
takes
\dot{h}_{2r}=(-1)^{r}\dot{\eta}_{2r}
to the
r
th
even complete symmetric function. This follows from the usual
infinite Grassmannian relation relating even complete symmetric functions to even elementary symmetric functions in
S\!ym

providing we can show that

(4.68)

\sum_{s=0}^{r}\dot{e}_{2s}\dot{h}_{2r-2s}=\delta_{r,0}

for all
r\geq 0
.
This is true because the sum on the left hand side is
\dot{z}_{2r}

by the first form of the definition
eq.

4.58
,
which we have already shown is zero for
r\geq 1
.
∎

Corollary 4.13
.

The largest supercommutative quotient of
OS\!ym_{n}
is

(4.69)

R_{n}:=OS\!ym_{n}\>\Big/\>\Big\langle\big(o^{(n)}\big)^{2},\big[o^{(n)},e_{2}^{(n)}\big]\Big\rangle.

The isomorphism
\theta
from
corollary

4.12
induces an isomorphism
\theta_{n}
from

R_{n}

to

S\!ym_{(n-1)/2}[c]
if
n
is odd,
or to the quotient of
S\!ym_{n/2}[c]
obtained by
setting the product of
c
and the
(n/2)
th even elementary symmetric polynomial to zero if
n
is even.

Proof.

This follows from
corollary

4.12
since a supercommutative quotient of
OS\!ym_{n}
is a supercommutative quotient of
OS\!ym

in which the images of all
e_{r}\>(r>n)
are zero.
∎

5.
Odd nil-Hecke algebras

This section is largely an exposition of results from
[
EKL
]
.
The
odd nil-Hecke algebra
is the graded superalgebra
ONH_{n}
with generators

x_{i}\>(i=1,\dots,n)
and
\tau_{j}\>(j=1,\dots,n-1)

which are odd of degrees 2 and
-2
, respectively,
subject to the following relations:

(5.1)

\displaystyle x_{i}x_{j}

\displaystyle=-x_{j}x_{i}

\displaystyle(i\neq j)

(5.2)

\displaystyle\tau_{i}\tau_{j}

\displaystyle=-\tau_{j}\tau_{i}

\displaystyle(|i-j|>1)

(5.3)

\displaystyle x_{i}\tau_{j}

\displaystyle=-\tau_{j}x_{i}

\displaystyle(i\neq j,j+1)

(5.4)

\displaystyle\tau_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=0

(5.5)

\displaystyle\tau_{j}\tau_{j+1}\tau_{j}

\displaystyle=-\tau_{j+1}\tau_{j}\tau_{j+1}

(5.6)

\displaystyle x_{i}\tau_{i}-\tau_{i}x_{i+1}

\displaystyle=1=\tau_{i}x_{i}-x_{i+1}\tau_{i}.

We warn the reader that the above is
not
the standard form of the presentation for this algebra which appears in all of the existing literature. The difference is in the relations
eqs.

5.5
and
5.6
, in which our minus signs become plus signs in the standard
presentation. To obtain the above presentation from the standard one, note that our generators
x_{i}
and
\tau_{j}
are equal to the elements denoted
(-1)^{i-1}x_{i}
and
(-1)^{j-1}\tau_{j}
elsewhere in the literature. This change certainly impacts many other formulae below, but it is usually straightforward to make the appropriate adaptation.
One advantage of our modified sign convention can already be seen in the definitions
eqs.

4.43
and
4.44
above—the corresponding formulae in
[
EKL
]
involve some additional signs.

Let
S\!_{n}
act on the left on

O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
by graded superalgebra automorphisms so that

{{}^{w}}\!x_{i}=(-1)^{\ell(w)+w(i)-i}x_{w(i)}
for
w\in S\!_{n},1\leq i\leq n
.
In particular:

(5.7)

{{}^{s_{j}}}x_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x_{j+1}&\text{if $i=j$}\\
x_{j}&\text{if $i=j+1$}\\
-x_{i}&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

The
odd Demazure operator

\partial_{j}:O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}\rightarrow O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}

is the linear map defined on
f\in O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
by

(5.8)

\partial_{j}(f)=\frac{(x_{j}+x_{j+1})f-\left({{}^{s_{j}}}f\right)(x_{j}+x_{j+1})}{x_{j}^{2}-x_{j+1}^{2}},

which makes sense because the denominator is central.
This formula first appeared in
[
KKO1
, (4.10)]
remembering, of course, our modified choice of signs. We actually never use this form of the definition of
\partial_{j}
, preferring the following
recursive definition:
\partial_{j}
is the unique odd linear map of degree
-2
such that

(5.9)

\displaystyle\partial_{j}(x_{i})

\displaystyle=\delta_{i,j}-\delta_{i,j+1},

\displaystyle\partial_{j}(fg)

\displaystyle=\partial_{j}(f)g+\left({{}^{s_{j}}}f\right)\partial_{j}(g)

for
f,g\in O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
.
Now we make the graded vector superspace
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}

into a left
ONH_{n}
-supermodule so that
x_{i}\in ONH_{n}
acts on
f\in O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
by
x_{i}\cdot f:=x_{i}f
, and
\tau_{j}\in ONH_{n}
acts by
\tau_{j}\cdot f:=\partial_{j}(f)
.
A tedious relation check shows that this definition makes sense.
It is straightforward to show by induction on
r
that

(5.10)

\displaystyle\tau_{i}\cdot x_{i}^{r+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{r}x_{i+1}^{s}x_{i}^{r-s},

\displaystyle\tau_{i}\cdot x_{i+1}^{r+1}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{s=0}^{r}x_{i}^{s}x_{i+1}^{r-s}.

One can rewrite

eq.

5.10
as the generating function identities:

(5.11)

\displaystyle\tau_{i}\cdot(t-x_{i})^{-1}

\displaystyle=(t-x_{i+1})^{-1}(t-x_{i})^{-1},

\displaystyle\tau_{i}\cdot(t-x_{i+1})^{-1}

\displaystyle=-(t-x_{i})^{-1}(t-x_{i+1})^{-1},

equalities in
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}\llbracket t^{-1}\rrbracket
.
From the former, we get that

(5.12)

\tau_{n-1}\cdots\tau_{1}\cdot(t-x_{1})^{-1}=(t-x_{n})^{-1}\cdots(t-x_{1})^{-1}.

Hence, recalling
eq.

4.56
, we get that

(5.13)

\tau_{n-1}\cdots\tau_{1}\cdot x_{1}^{n+r-1}=h^{(n)}_{r}

on computing
t^{-n-r}
-coefficients.

By the relations
eqs.

5.1
,
5.4
,
5.2
,
5.5
,
5.3
and
5.6
,
ONH_{n}
admits an algebra involution
{\gamma}_{n}
and a superalgebra anti-involution
*

defined by

(5.14)

\displaystyle{\gamma}_{n}:ONH_{n}

\displaystyle\rightarrow ONH_{n},

\displaystyle x_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto x_{n+1-i},

\displaystyle\tau_{j}

\displaystyle\mapsto-\tau_{n-j},

(5.15)

\displaystyle*:ONH_{n}

\displaystyle\rightarrow ONH_{n},

\displaystyle x_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto x_{i},

\displaystyle\tau_{j}

\displaystyle\mapsto-\tau_{j}.

These definitions are consistent with
eqs.

4.41
and
4.42
.
Note also that
{\gamma}_{n}
and
*
commute with each other.
Mirroring the notation for symmetric groups from
General conventions
, there is also a homomorphism

(5.16)

\operatorname{sh}_{n}:ONH_{n^{\prime}}\rightarrow ONH_{n+n^{\prime}},\qquad x_{i}\mapsto x_{i+n},\tau_{j}\mapsto\tau_{j+n}.

We will show shortly that this is injective, but this is not yet clear.
Similarly, there is a homomorphism

\operatorname{sh}_{n}:O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n^{\prime}}\rightarrow O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n+n^{\prime}},x_{i}\mapsto x_{i+n}
, which is obviously injective.
We have that

(5.17)

\displaystyle{\gamma}_{n}(a)\cdot{\gamma}_{n}(f)

\displaystyle={\gamma}_{n}(a\cdot f),

\displaystyle\operatorname{sh}_{n}(a)\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{n}(f)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{sh}_{n}(a\cdot f),

for
a\in ONH_{n}
,
f\in O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
,
or
a\in ONH_{n^{\prime}}
,
f\in O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n^{\prime}}
, respectively.

For each
w\in S\!_{n}
, we pick a reduced expression
w=s_{j_{1}}\cdots s_{j_{l}}
then set
\tau_{w}:=\tau_{j_{1}}\cdots\tau_{j_{l}}
. For the longest
element
w_{n}
, we choose the reduced expression

(s_{n-1}s_{n-2}\cdots s_{1})(s_{n-1}s_{n-2}\cdots s_{2})\cdots(s_{n-1}s_{n-2})s_{n-1}
,
and adopt the shorthands

(5.18)

\displaystyle\omega_{n}

\displaystyle:=\tau_{w_{n}}=\tau_{n-1}\tau_{n-2}\cdots\tau_{1}\operatorname{sh}_{1}(\omega_{n-1}),

\displaystyle\xi_{n}

\displaystyle:=x_{n-1}x_{n-2}^{2}\cdots x_{1}^{n-1}=\operatorname{sh}_{1}(\xi_{n-1})x_{1}^{n-1}.

These elements have the following desirable property.

Lemma 5.1
.

\omega_{n}\cdot\xi_{n}=1
.

Proof.

When
n=1
, this is clear as
\omega_{n}=1=\xi_{n}
.
The result for
n>1
follows by induction:

\displaystyle\omega_{n}\cdot\xi_{n}

\displaystyle=\tau_{n-1}\cdots\tau_{1}\operatorname{sh}_{1}(\omega_{n-1})\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{1}(\xi_{n-1})x_{1}^{n-1}

\displaystyle=\tau_{n-1}\cdots\tau_{1}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{1}(\omega_{n-1}\cdot\xi_{n-1})x_{1}^{n-1}=\tau_{n-1}\cdots\tau_{1}\cdot x_{1}^{n-1}=1,

using
eq.

5.13
for the final equality.
∎

It is also clear that

(5.19)

\displaystyle{\gamma}_{n}(\omega_{n})

\displaystyle=\zeta_{n}\omega_{n}

for some
\zeta_{n}\in\{\pm 1\}
.
One can verify explicitly
that
\zeta_{n}=(-1)^{\binom{n+1}{3}}
; the calculation is similar to the proof of
[
EKL
, Lem. 3.2]
. However, the only place this sign is used is in the proof of
theorem

6.12
, and in that place we actually do not need to know its acual value.

An important role will be played by the
odd Schubert polynomials

(5.20)

p^{(n)}_{w}:=\tau_{w^{-1}w_{n}}\cdot\xi_{n}\in O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}.

For example, we have that

p^{(3)}_{1}=1
,

p^{(3)}_{s_{1}}=-x_{1}
,

p^{(3)}_{s_{2}}=x_{1}+x_{2}
,

p^{(3)}_{s_{2}s_{1}}=x_{1}^{2}
,

p^{(3)}_{s_{1}s_{2}}=-x_{2}x_{1}
and

p^{(3)}_{s_{2}s_{1}s_{2}}=x_{2}x_{1}^{2}
.
In general,
p^{(n)}_{w}
depends up to sign on the choice of reduced expression for
w^{-1}w_{n}
,
but we always have that
p^{(n)}_{w_{n}}=\xi_{n}
and
p^{(n)}_{1}=1
thanks to
lemma

5.1
.
Note also that
\operatorname{deg}\left(p^{(n)}_{w}\right)=2\ell(w)
and
\operatorname{par}\left(p^{(n)}_{w}\right)\equiv\ell(w)\pmod{2}
.

Theorem 5.2

(
[
EKL
, Prop. 2.11]
)
.

The elements
\big\{x^{\kappa}\tau_{w}=x_{1}^{\kappa_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\kappa_{n}}\tau_{w}\>\big|\>w\in S\!_{n},\kappa\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\big\}
give a basis for
ONH_{n}
. Moreover,
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
is a faithful
ONH_{n}
-module.

Proof.

First one shows using
eqs.

5.4
,
5.2
and
5.5

that any word in
\tau_{j}\>(j=1,\dots,n-1)
can be reduced to
0
or
\pm\tau_{w}
for some
w\in S\!_{n}
. It follows that the set in
theorem

5.2
spans
ONH_{n}
. Then to establish the linear independence,
suppose that we have some non-trivial linear relation

\sum_{w\in S\!_{n}}\sum_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}c_{w,\kappa}x^{\kappa}\tau_{w}=0

between the elements of this set.
Pick
w
of minimal length such that
c_{w,\kappa}\neq 0
for some
\kappa
.
Then we act on
p^{(n)}_{w}
.
For
w^{\prime}\neq w
with
\ell(w^{\prime})\geq\ell(w)
, we have that
\tau_{w^{\prime}}\cdot p^{(n)}_{w}=0

by the relations
eqs.

5.4
,
5.2
and
5.5
, and
\tau_{w}\cdot p^{(n)}_{w}=\pm 1
by
lemma

5.1
.
So we deduce that
\sum_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}c_{w,\kappa}x^{\kappa}=0
,
which is a contradiction. This also shows
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
is faithful.
∎

Corollary 5.3
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}ONH_{n}=\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}OS\!ym_{n}\times q^{\binom{n}{2}}[n]^{!}_{q,\pi}\times q^{-\binom{n}{2}}\overline{[n]}_{q,\pi}^{!},

Proof.

The theorem gives that

\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}ONH_{n}=\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}\times\sum_{w\in S\!_{n}}(\pi q^{2})^{-\ell(w)}.

Now replace
\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
by the first formula for it from
corollary

4.8
,
and replace the summation by a product using
eq.

3.3
.
∎

The basis theorem just established
implies that the obvious homomorphism
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}\rightarrow ONH_{n}
is injective.
Henceforth, we identify
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}

with a subalgebra of
ONH_{n}
via this map.
Another application of the basis theorem shows that the
homomorphism
ONH_{n}\hookrightarrow ONH_{n+1}
taking
x_{i}
to
x_{i}
and
\tau_{j}
to
\tau_{j}
is injective.
Thus, we have a tower of graded superalgebras

ONH_{0}\subset ONH_{1}\subset ONH_{2}\subset\cdots
.
The basis theorem also shows that the homomorphism

\operatorname{sh}_{n}:ONH_{n^{\prime}}\rightarrow ONH_{n+n^{\prime}}

from
eq.

5.16
is injective, as promised earlier.
For
\alpha\in\Lambda(k,n)
, we let
ONH_{\alpha}
be the subalgebra

\big\{x^{\kappa}\tau_{w}\>\big|\>w\in S\!_{\alpha},\kappa\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\big\}
of
ONH_{n}
.
Finally,
let

ONH^{{\operatorname{fin}}}_{n}
be the subalgebra of
ONH_{n}
with basis
\{\tau_{w}\>|\>w\in S\!_{n}\}
. As an algebra,
ONH^{\operatorname{fin}}_{n}
is generated by the elements

\tau_{j}\>(j=1,\dots,n-1)
subject just to
eqs.

5.4
,
5.2
and
5.5
. There is a unique way to make the ground field
\mathbb{F}

into a purely even graded left
ONH^{\operatorname{fin}}_{n}
-supermodule concentrated in degree 0; each
\tau_{j}
acts as zero.
There is then a canonical isomorphism of graded
ONH_{n}
-supermodules

(5.21)

ONH_{n}\otimes_{ONH^{\operatorname{fin}}_{n}}\mathbb{F}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits}}O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n},\qquad x^{\kappa}\otimes 1\mapsto x^{\kappa}.

This isomorphism explains the origin of the polynomial representation of
ONH_{n}
.

Now recall the subalgebra
OS\!ym_{n}
of
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
which was defined just after
theorem

4.4
—it is the subalgebra of
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
generated by the odd symmetric polynomials
e_{r}^{(n)}
from
eq.

4.43
.
A different formulation of the definition of
OS\!ym_{n}
was adopted in
[
EKL
]
,
where
OS\!ym_{n}
was defined from the outset to be
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n-1}\ker\partial_{i}
, which is a subalgebra of
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
.
We will deduce the equality of
OS\!ym_{n}
with this subalgebra in
corollary

5.5
, but one containment is obvious:
we have that

(5.22)

\displaystyle OS\!ym_{n}\subseteq\bigcap_{i=1}^{n-1}\ker\partial_{i}.

To see this, it suffices to check that
\partial_{i}(e_{r}^{(n)})=0
for all
i
and
r=1,\dots,n
, which follows from the definitions since
\partial_{i}(x_{i}+x_{i+1})=\partial_{i}(x_{i}x_{i+1})=0
.

Theorem 5.4

(
[
EKL
, Prop. 2.13, Cor. 2.14]
)
.

The graded right
OS\!ym_{n}
-supermodule
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
is free of
graded rank
q^{\binom{n}{2}}[n]^{!}_{q,\pi}
with basis

\big\{p^{(n)}_{w}\>|\>w\in S\!_{n}\}

given by the odd Schubert polynomials from
eq.

5.20
. So we
have that

(5.23)

O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}=\bigoplus_{w\in S\!_{n}}p_{w}^{(n)}OS\!ym_{n}\qquad\text{ with }\qquad p_{w}^{(n)}OS\!ym_{n}\simeq(\Pi Q^{2})^{\ell(w)}OS\!ym_{n}

as graded right
OS\!ym_{n}
-supermodules. Moreover, the action of
ONH_{n}
on
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
induces a graded superalgebra isomorphism

(5.24)

ONH_{n}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits}}\End_{{\text{-}}OS\!ym_{n}}(O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}).

Proof.

We claim that the polynomials
p^{(n)}_{w}\>(w\in S\!_{n})
are linearly independent over
OS\!ym_{n}
. To see this, take a non-trivial linear relation

\sum_{w\in S\!_{n}}p^{(n)}_{w}b_{w}=0

for
b_{w}\in OS\!ym_{n}
. Choose
w
of maximal length such that
b_{w}\neq 0
. Then we act with
\tau_{w}
.
We have that
\tau_{w}\cdot p^{(n)}_{w^{\prime}}b_{w^{\prime}}=0
for
w^{\prime}\neq w
by the relations
eqs.

5.4
,
5.2
and
5.5
,
and
\tau_{w}\cdot p^{(n)}_{w}b_{w}=\pm b_{w}
, so we deduce that

b_{w}=0
, a contradiction.
The claim implies that the
OS\!ym_{n}
-submodule of
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
generated by
p^{(n)}_{w}\>(w\in S\!_{n})

is of graded superdimension
\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}OS\!ym_{n}\times\sum_{w\in S\!_{n}}(\pi q^{2})^{\ell(w)}
, which is equal to

\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
by
corollary

4.8
.
Hence, the
p^{(n)}_{w}\>(w\in S\!_{n})
also span
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
as an
OS\!ym_{n}
-module,
and we have proved
eq.

5.23
.

To establish
eq.

5.24
, we first note by
eq.

5.23
that

\displaystyle\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}\End_{{\text{-}}OS\!ym_{n}}(O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,y\in S\!_{n}}(\pi q^{2})^{\ell(x)-\ell(y)}=\left(\sum_{x\in S\!_{n}}(\pi q^{2})^{\ell(x)}\right)\left(\sum_{y\in S\!_{n}}(\pi q^{2})^{-\ell(y)}\right)=q^{\binom{n}{2}}[n]^{!}_{q,\pi}\times q^{-\binom{n}{2}}\overline{[n]}^{!}_{\!q,\pi},

applying
eq.

3.3
.
The homomorphism
\rho:ONH_{n}\rightarrow\End_{{\text{-}}OS\!ym_{n}}(O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n})
is injective by

theorem

5.2
.
Therefore it is an isomorphism because the graded superdimensions
are the same thanks to
corollary

5.3
.
∎

Corollary 5.5
.

We have that
\displaystyle OS\!ym_{n}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n-1}\ker\partial_{i}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n-1}\im\partial_{i}.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
\ker\partial_{i}=\im\partial_{i}
for each
i
, hence, the second equality holds.
For the first one, we have already noted in
eq.

5.22
that
OS\!ym_{n}\subseteq\bigcap_{i=1}^{n-1}\ker\partial_{i}
.
Conversely, take
f\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{n-1}\ker\partial_{i}
and write it as

f=\sum_{w\in S\!_{n}}p^{(n)}_{w}b_{w}
for
b_{w}\in OS\!ym_{n}
.
We need to show that
b_{w}=0
except when
w=1
.
Suppose for a contradiction that this is not the case, and pick
w
of maximal length such that
b_{w}\neq 0
. Then we act on
f
with
\tau_{w}

to see that
b_{w}=0
, contradiction.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

As well as the basis
F:=\big\{p_{w}^{(n)}\>\big|\>w\in S\!_{n}\big\}
of odd Schubert polynomials from
theorem

5.4
, the monomials
G:=\big\{x_{n}^{\kappa_{n}}\cdots x_{1}^{\kappa_{1}}\>\big|\>\kappa\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\text{ with }0\leq\kappa_{i}\leq n-i\big\}

form a basis for
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
as a free right
OS\!ym_{n}
-module. To see this, it suffices to show that
\mathbb{F}F=\mathbb{F}G
.
The elements of
F
are linearly independent over
\mathbb{F}
by
theorem

5.4
, so
\dim\mathbb{F}F=n!
. Also
\dim\mathbb{F}G=n!
obviously. So we are reduced to checking that
\mathbb{F}F\subseteq\mathbb{F}G
. To see this, we note first that
\mathbb{F}G
is invariant under the
action of each
\tau_{i}
, as may be seen directly using
eq.

5.10

plus
\tau_{i}\cdot x_{i+1}^{r}x_{i}^{r}=0
.
Since
\xi_{n}\in\mathbb{F}G
, it follows that
p_{w}^{(n)}=\tau_{w^{-1}w_{n}}\cdot\xi_{n}\in\mathbb{F}G
for each
w\in S\!_{n}
as claimed.

Let
M_{q^{\binom{n}{2}}[n]^{!}_{q,\pi}}(OS\!ym_{n})
denote the usual algebra of matrices
A=(a_{w,w^{\prime}})_{w,w^{\prime}\in S\!_{n}}
with entries in
OS\!ym_{n}
viewed as a graded superalgebra so that the matrix with
a\in(OS\!ym_{n})_{i,p}
in its
(w,w^{\prime})
-entry and zeros elsewhere is of degree
i+2\ell(w)-2\ell(w^{\prime})
and parity
p+\ell(w)-\ell(w^{\prime})\pmod{2}
. This graded superalgebra may be identified with
\End_{{\text{-}}OS\!ym_{n}}(O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n})
so that the matrix
A
just described corresponds to the unique right
OS\!ym_{n}
-supermodule endomorphism of
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
taking
p_{w^{\prime}}^{(n)}
to
\sum_{w\in S\!_{n}}p_{w}^{(n)}a_{w,w^{\prime}}
for every
w^{\prime}\in S\!_{n}
. Thus,
theorem

5.4
shows that
ONH_{n}\cong M_{q^{\binom{n}{2}}[n]^{!}_{q,\pi}}(OS\!ym_{n})
. It follows that the graded superfunctors

(5.25)

\displaystyle-\otimes_{ONH_{n}}O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}

\displaystyle:{\operatorname{gsMod-}}ONH_{n}\rightarrow{\operatorname{gsMod-}}OS\!ym_{n},

(5.26)

\displaystyle\Hom_{ONH_{n}}(O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n},-)

\displaystyle:ONH_{n}{\operatorname{-gsMod}}\rightarrow OS\!ym_{n}{\operatorname{-gsMod}}

are equivalences of graded
(Q,\Pi)
-supercategories.

Theorem 5.7

(
[
EKL
, Prop. 2.15]
)
.

The even center
Z(ONH_{n})_{{\bar{0}}}
of
ONH_{n}
is the
graded algebra consisting of symmetric polynomials in
x_{1}^{2},\dots,x_{n}^{2}
. This coincides with the even center
Z(OS\!ym_{n})_{{\bar{0}}}
of
OS\!ym_{n}
embedded into
ONH_{n}
in the natural way.

Proof.

This is proved in
[
EKL
]
but
we give a slightly different argument since there are some minor issues in the first paragraph of the original proof, which does not restrict attention to the
even
center.
Take
z\in Z(ONH_{n})
. Using
theorem

5.2
, we have that

z=\sum_{w\in S\!_{n}}f_{w}\tau_{w}

for unique
f_{w}\in O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
. The first step is to show that
f_{w}=0
unless
w=1
. To see this, suppose for a contradiction that it is not the case. Let
w
be of maximal length such that
f_{w}\neq 0
.
Pick
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
j:=w(i)\neq i
. We have that

x_{i}z=\sum_{w\in S\!_{n}}x_{i}f_{w}\tau_{w}.

Now we use the relations to express
zx_{i}
as a linear combination
\sum_{v\in S\!_{n}}g_{v}\tau_{v}
for
g_{v}\in O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
.
Since
\tau_{w}x_{i}=\pm x_{j}\tau_{w}
plus a linear combination of
\tau_{w^{\prime}}
for
w^{\prime}
with
\ell(w^{\prime})<\ell(w)
,
we see that
g_{w}=\pm f_{w}x_{j}
. Thus, we must have that
x_{i}f_{w}=\pm f_{w}x_{j}
.
Since
i\neq j
, it is easy to see that this implies that
f_{w}=0
. So now we have proved that
z\in O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
.
Next,
assuming also that
z
is even
, we show that
z
is in fact in
\mathbb{F}[x_{1}^{2},\dots,x_{n}^{2}]
essentially following the idea from the proof in
[
EKL
]
. Take any
1\leq i\leq n
and suppose that
z=\sum_{k\geq 0}f_{k}x_{i}^{k}
for
f_{k}
belonging to the subalgebra of
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
generated by
x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n}
. Since
x_{i}z=zx_{i}
, we get that each
f_{k}
must be even.
Since
z
is even too it follows that
f_{k}=0
unless
k
is even. This shows that
z
only involves even powers of
x_{i}
. This is true for each
i
, so
z\in\mathbb{F}[x_{1}^{2},\dots,x_{n}^{2}]
as claimed.
To complete the proof that
z
is actually a symmetric polynomial in
x_{1}^{2},\dots,x_{n}^{2}
, and to show that any such polynomial is central, we can now refer the reader to the argument given in the second two paragraphs of the proof of
[
EKL
, Prop. 2.15]
.

Finally we explain how to see that
Z(ONH_{n})_{{\bar{0}}}
coincides with
Z(OS\!ym_{n})_{{\bar{0}}}
.
The supercenter of the matrix algebra
M_{q^{\binom{n}{2}}[n]^{!}_{q,\pi}}(OS\!ym_{n})
is isomorphic to
Z(OS\!ym_{n})
via the map
taking
z\in Z(OS\!ym_{n})
to the matrix

\operatorname{diag}(z,\dots,z)
. It follows that the even centers are isomorphic too.
Given
z
in the even center of
ONH_{n}
, we have just shown that it is a polynomial
in
x_{1}^{2},\dots,x_{n}^{2}
, so we have that
zp_{w}^{(n)}=p_{w}^{(n)}z
for all
w\in S\!_{n}
. It follows that
z
acts on
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
in the same way as the matrix
\operatorname{diag}(z,\dots,z)
under the identification of
ONH_{n}
with matrices described above. This shows that the natural embedding of
OS\!ym_{n}
into
ONH_{n}

restricts to give an isomorphism between the even centers of
OS\!ym_{n}
and
ONH_{n}
.
∎

The idempotents in
ONH_{n}
corresponding to the diagonal matrix units
e_{w,w}\in M_{q^{\binom{n}{2}}[n]^{!}_{q,\pi}}(OS\!ym_{n})
,
that is, the elements which act on
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
as the projections onto the indecomposable summands in
eq.

5.23
, give a complete set of primitive idempotents in
ONH_{n}
.
It is clear from
theorem

5.2
that the component of
ONH_{n}
of
smallest degree is 1-dimensional
spanned by
\omega_{n}
. Since
\omega_{n}\xi_{n}\omega_{n}
is of the same degree as
\omega_{n}
, it follows that

\omega_{n}\xi_{n}\omega_{n}
is a scalar multiple of
\omega_{n}
.
Moreover, both
\omega_{n}\xi_{n}\omega_{n}
and
\omega_{n}

map
\xi_{n}
to
1
by
lemma

5.1
, hence, we actually have that

(5.27)

\omega_{n}\xi_{n}\omega_{n}=\omega_{n}.

From this it follows that the following are both idempotents:

(5.28)

\displaystyle(\xi\omega)_{n}

\displaystyle:=\xi_{n}\omega_{n},

\displaystyle(\omega\xi)_{n}

\displaystyle:=\omega_{n}\xi_{n}.

The first of these,
(\xi\omega)_{n}
,
is exactly the matrix unit
e_{w_{n},w_{n}}
which projects
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
onto the top degree component
p_{w_{n}}^{(n)}OS\!ym_{n}
.
This follows almost immediately since
lemma

5.1
shows that

\xi_{n}\omega_{n}\cdot p_{w_{n}}^{(n)}=p_{w_{n}}^{(n)}
and
\xi_{n}\omega_{n}\cdot p_{w}^{(n)}=0
for all other
w\in S\!_{n}
as
\omega_{n}\cdot p_{w}^{(n)}=0
by degree considerations.
In particular, this shows that
(\xi\omega)_{n}
is a primitive idempotent.
The second one,
(\omega\xi)_{n}
, is also primitive
since we have that

(5.29)

(\omega\xi)_{n}=(\xi\omega)_{n}^{*}.

To see this, it is clear from the definitions that

(\xi\omega)_{n}^{*}=\pm(\omega\xi)_{n}
, and the sign must be plus since

(\omega\xi)_{n}
is an idempotent.
Note also that
(\omega\xi)_{n}O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}=OS\!ym_{n}
.
To see this, every
\partial_{i}
annihilates

(\omega\xi_{n})\cdot O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
, so
(\omega\xi)_{n}\cdot O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}\subseteq OS\!ym_{n}
thanks to
corollary

5.5
, and
it is easy to see directly that

(\omega\xi)_{n}\cdot f=f
for any
f\in OS\!ym_{n}

giving the other containment.
Thus, we have shown that

(5.30)

\displaystyle(\xi\omega)_{n}\cdot O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}

\displaystyle=\xi_{n}OS\!ym_{n},

\displaystyle(\omega\xi)_{n}\cdot O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}

\displaystyle=OS\!ym_{n}.

For
n\geq 2
,
(\omega\xi)_{n}
is
not
the idempotent corresponding to the matrix unit
e_{1,1}
in the matrix algebra
M_{q^{\binom{n}{2}}[n]^{!}_{q,\pi}}(OS\!ym_{n})
,
i.e., it is a projection of
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
onto
OS\!ym_{n}
, but along a different direct sum decomposition to
eq.

5.23
.
It is convenient to work with since
left multiplication by
\omega_{n}
defines a homogeneous isomorphism
(\xi\omega)_{n}\cdot O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits}}(\omega\xi)_{n}\cdot O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
, with inverse defined by left multiplication by
\xi_{n}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

We have that

\displaystyle ONH_{n}\simeq\bigoplus_{w\in S\!_{n}}(\Pi Q^{2})^{\ell(w)}\>(\omega\xi)_{n}ONH_{n}\simeq\bigoplus_{w\in S\!_{n}}(\Pi Q^{2})^{-\ell(w)}\>(\xi\omega)_{n}ONH_{n}
as a graded right
ONH_{n}
-supermodule.

Proof.

Left multiplication by
\omega_{n}
defines an isomorphism
(\xi\omega)_{n}ONH_{n}\simeq(\Pi Q^{2})^{\binom{n}{2}}(\omega\xi)_{n}ONH_{n}
with inverse given by left multiplication by
\xi_{n}
. Therefore it suffices to prove the first isomorphism. Since
eq.

5.25
is a graded superequivalence, we can apply it to reduce the problem to proving that

O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}\simeq\bigoplus_{w\in S\!_{n}}(\Pi Q^{2})^{\ell(w)}\>OS\!ym_{n}

as graded right
OS\!ym_{n}
-supermodules, where we have used that
(\omega\xi)_{n}O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}=OS\!ym_{n}
by
eq.

5.30
.
This follows from
eq.

5.23
.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

The map
\imath:O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}\rightarrow ONH_{n}(\omega\xi)_{n},f\mapsto f(\omega\xi)_{n}
is an even degree 0 isomorphism of graded left
ONH_{n}
-supermodules.
The map
\jmath:OS\!ym_{n}\rightarrow(\omega\xi)_{n}ONH_{n}(\omega\xi)_{n}

defined by the composition of the natural inclusion of
OS\!ym_{n}
into
ONH_{n}
followed by
the projection
a\mapsto(\omega\xi)_{n}a(\omega\xi)_{n}

is a graded superalgebra isomorphism.
Moreover, we have that
\imath(fa)=\imath(f)\jmath(a)
for all
f\in O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
and
a\in OS\!ym_{n}
.

Proof.

Since
\tau_{j}(\omega\xi)_{n}=0
by degree considerations, there is a unique graded left
ONH_{n}
-supermodule homomorphism
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}\rightarrow ONH_{n}(\omega\xi)_{n}
taking
1
to
(\omega\xi)_{n}
thanks to
eq.

5.21
.
This is
\imath
. Also
(\omega\xi)_{n}\cdot 1=1
, so there is a supermodule homomorphism

ONH_{n}(\omega\xi)_{n}\rightarrow O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n},a\mapsto a\cdot 1
. These two maps are mutual inverses, hence,

\imath
is an isomorphism.

The restriction of
\imath
gives an isomorphism
(\omega\xi)_{n}\cdot O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits}}(\omega\xi)_{n}O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}(\omega\xi)_{n}
.
Since
(\omega\xi)_{n}a(\omega\xi)_{n}=a(\omega\xi)_{n}
for
a\in OS\!ym_{n}
and
(\omega\xi)_{n}\cdot O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}=OS\!ym_{n}
by
eq.

5.30
, this restriction is the isomorphism
\jmath

from the statement of the lemma.
∎

Corollary 5.10
.

Using
\jmath
to identify
OS\!ym_{n}
with
(\omega\xi)_{n}ONH_{n}(\omega\xi)_{n}
, the superfunctors

-\otimes_{ONH_{n}}O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}

and
\Hom_{ONH_{n}}(O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n},-)

from
eqs.

5.25
and
5.26
are isomorphic to the idempotent truncation functors defined by right and left multiplication by the idempotent

(\omega\xi)_{n}
, respectively.

We note finally that there is also a
right
action
of
ONH_{n}
on
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
, and everything in this section
could be reformulated in terms of this viewed as an
(OS\!ym_{n},ONH_{n})
-superbimdule.
This right action may be defined succinctly from

(5.31)

f\cdot a:=(-1)^{\operatorname{par}(a)\operatorname{par}(f)}\big(a^{*}\cdot f^{*}\big)^{*}

for
f\in O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n},a\in ONH_{n}
.
The following more explicit description similar to
eq.

5.9

can easily be derived from this:

(5.32)

\displaystyle x_{i}\cdot\tau_{j}

\displaystyle=\delta_{i,j}-\delta_{i,j+1},

\displaystyle(fg)\cdot\tau_{j}

\displaystyle=f(g\cdot\tau_{j})+(f\cdot\tau_{j})\left({{}^{s_{j}}}g\right).

for
f,g\in O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
.
The right action of
ONH_{n}

on
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
obviously commutes with the natural
action of
OS\!ym_{n}
by left multiplication.

theorem

5.4
and
eq.

5.29
imply that

(5.33)

O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}\simeq\bigoplus_{w\in S_{n}}(\Pi Q^{2})^{\ell(w)}OS\!ym_{n}

as a graded left
OS\!ym_{n}
-supermodule,
with the “bottom” summand that is
OS\!ym_{n}
itself
being the image of the
idempotent
(\xi\omega)_{n}
acting on the right.
We stress that the action
eq.

5.31
is different
from the right action defined via

f\cdot a:=(-1)^{\operatorname{par}(a)\operatorname{par}(f)}a^{*}\cdot f
; the latter
action does not commute with the left action of
OS\!ym_{n}
.
When we talk about
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
as a right
ONH_{n}
-supermodule,
we always mean the action defined via
eq.

5.31
.

Lemma 5.11
.

\displaystyle(t+x_{2})^{-1}x_{1}^{r}\cdot\tau_{1}=(t+x_{2})^{-1}x_{2}^{r}\Big(t+(-1)^{r}x_{1}\Big)^{-1}+\sum_{q=0}^{r-1}(-1)^{qr}(t+x_{2})^{-1}x_{2}^{q}x_{1}^{r-q-1}.

Proof.

Similarly to
eqs.

5.10
and
5.11
, one shows that
(t+x_{2})^{-1}\cdot\tau_{1}=(t+x_{2})^{-1}(t+x_{1})^{-1}
and
x_{1}^{r}\cdot\tau_{1}=\sum_{q=0}^{r-1}x_{1}^{r-q-1}x_{2}^{q}
.
These combine using
eq.

5.32

to give the final formula; one also needs to commute all
x_{2}

to the left of all
x_{1}
producing some additional signs.
∎

6.
Odd Schur polynomials

Another important basis of
OS\!ym
is introduced in
[
EK
, Sec. 3.3]
: the basis of

odd Schur functions

\{s_{\lambda}\>|\>\lambda\in{\Lambda^{\!+}}\}
.
As explained after
[
EK
, Cor. 3.9]
,
this is the basis of
OS\!ym
characterized uniquely by the properties
that
(s_{\lambda},h_{\mu})^{-}=0
if
\mu>_{{\operatorname{lex}}}\lambda

and
s_{\lambda}=h_{\lambda}+
(a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of
other
h_{\mu}
for
\mu>_{{\operatorname{lex}}}\lambda
).
Some examples can be found in the appendix of
[
EK
]
.
The key property of odd Schur functions is that they are
signed-orthonormal:

Theorem 6.1

(
[
EK
, Cor. 3.9]
)
.

For
\lambda,\mu\in{\Lambda^{\!+}}
, we have that

(s_{\lambda},s_{\mu})^{-}=(-1)^{dN(\lambda)}\delta_{\lambda,\mu}
.

The
odd Kostka matrix

(K_{\lambda,\mu})_{\lambda,\mu\in{\Lambda^{\!+}}}

is the transition matrix defined from

(6.1)

h_{\mu}=\sum_{\lambda\in{\Lambda^{\!+}}}K_{\lambda,\mu}s_{\lambda}.

There is an explicit formula for the entries of this matrix
derived in
[
EK
, (3.7)]
, as follows.
For a
\lambda
-tableau
T
(
=
a function from the Young diagram of
\lambda
to
\mathbb{Z}
), we let
N(T)
be the number of pairs of boxes
(A,B)
such that
B
is strictly
north of
A
and also
T(B)\geq T(A)
.
For example, if
T
is the
unique semistandard
\lambda
-tableau of content
\lambda
(so all entries on row
i
are equal to
i
) then
N(T)=0
.
Then

(6.2)

K_{\lambda,\mu}=\sum_{T}(-1)^{N(T)}

summing over semistandard
\lambda
-tableaux
T
of content
\mu
.
Note from this description
that
K_{\lambda,\mu}=0
unless
\lambda\geq\mu
in the dominance order.
So we actually have that

(6.3)

s_{\lambda}=h_{\lambda}+\text{(a $\mathbb{Z}$-linear combination of
other $h_{\mu}$ for $\mu>\lambda$)}

in the dominance rather than merely lexicographic ordering.

Since the involution
\psi_{1}\psi_{2}
in
[
EK
]

is our
\psi
by
remark

4.3
,

[
EK
, Lem. 3.11]
shows that

(6.4)

\psi(s_{\lambda})=(-1)^{NE(\lambda)+|\lambda|}s_{\lambda^{\mathtt{t}}}

for any
\lambda\in{\Lambda^{\!+}}
.
Hence, applying
\psi
to
eq.

6.3
, we have that

(6.5)

s_{\lambda^{\mathtt{t}}}=(-1)^{NE(\lambda)}e_{\lambda}+\text{(a $\mathbb{Z}$-linear combination of
other $e_{\mu}$ for $\mu>\lambda$)}.

From
eqs.

6.3
and
6.5
, we see in particular that

(6.6)

\displaystyle s_{(r)}

\displaystyle=h_{r},

\displaystyle s_{(1^{r})}

\displaystyle=e_{r}.

Using also
eqs.

6.4
and
4.34
,

theorem

6.1
implies:

Corollary 6.2
.

For
\lambda,\mu\in{\Lambda^{\!+}}
, we have that

(s_{\lambda},s_{\mu})^{+}=(-1)^{dE(\lambda)}\delta_{\lambda,\mu}
.

Applying
eq.

6.4
one more time,
this time combined with the first identity from
eq.

4.34
,
it follows that

s_{\lambda}
can also be characterized as the unique element of

OS\!ym
such that
\big(s_{\lambda},e_{\mu}\big)^{+}=0
for
\mu>_{{\operatorname{lex}}}\lambda^{\mathtt{t}}

and
s_{\lambda}=(-1)^{NE(\lambda)}e_{\lambda^{\mathtt{t}}}+
(a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of
e_{\mu}
for
\mu>_{\operatorname{lex}}\lambda^{\mathtt{t}}
).
This characterization plus
lemma

4.2
and
the second identity from
eq.

4.34
implies that

(6.7)

{\gamma}(s_{\lambda})^{*}=(-1)^{dN(\lambda)+dE(\lambda)}s_{\lambda}.

We define the
dual odd Schur function

(6.8)

\sigma_{\lambda}:={\gamma}(s_{\lambda})=(-1)^{dN(\lambda)+dE(\lambda)}s_{\lambda}^{*}.

In particular, applying

{\gamma}
to
eq.

6.6
and using the definitions
eqs.

4.48
and
4.49
, we have that

(6.9)

\displaystyle\sigma_{(r)}

\displaystyle=\eta_{r},

\displaystyle\sigma_{(1^{r})}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{r}.

The
odd Schur polynomial

s^{(n)}_{\lambda}
and
the
dual odd Schur polynomial

\sigma^{(n)}_{\lambda}

are the images of
s_{\lambda}

and
\sigma_{\lambda}

under the quotient map
\pi_{n}:OS\!ym\rightarrow OS\!ym_{n}
, respectively.
The dual odd Schur polynomials coincide with the polynomials
introduced in
[
EKL
, Def. 4.10]
and
play an important role in
theorem

6.12
below.

Theorem 6.3
.

The set
\big\{s^{(n)}_{\lambda}\>\big|\>\lambda\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{n}}\big\}
is a basis for
OS\!ym_{n}
. Moreover, for any
\lambda\in{\Lambda^{\!+}}
, we have that

(6.10)

s_{\lambda}^{(n)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}x^{\lambda}+\text{(a $\mathbb{Z}$-linear combination of $x^{\kappa}$ for
$\kappa\in\mathbb{N}^{n}$ with $\kappa<\lambda$)}&\text{if ${\operatorname{ht}}(\lambda)\leq n$}\\
0&\text{if ${\operatorname{ht}}(\lambda)>n$.}\end{array}\right.

Proof.

This follows from
eqs.

6.5
and
4.5
plus
section

4
.
∎

Corollary 6.4
.

The set
\big\{\sigma^{(n)}_{\lambda}\>\big|\>\lambda\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{n}}\big\}
is a basis for
OS\!ym_{n}
. Moreover,

\sigma^{(n)}_{\lambda}=0
for
\lambda\in{\Lambda^{\!+}}
with
{\operatorname{ht}}(\lambda)>n
.

Proof.

Apply
{\gamma}_{n}
to the results established in the theorem.
∎

Corollary 6.5
.

The set
\big\{h^{(n)}_{\lambda}\>\big|\>\lambda\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{n}}\big\}
is a basis for
OS\!ym_{n}
.

Proof.

By graded dimension considerations, it suffices to show that

\big\{h^{(n)}_{\lambda}\>\big|\>\lambda\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{n}}\big\}

spans
OS\!ym_{n}
. This follows from the theorem
using
eq.

6.3

and also the observation that

\mu>\lambda\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{n}}\Rightarrow\mu\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{n}}
.
∎

The next result was originally formulated as a conjecture in
[
EKL
, Conj. 5.3]
, and the conjecture was proved in
[
E
, Th. 3.8]
.
However, we also need to reformulate it using our sign conventions, and for this we need a preliminary lemma.

Lemma 6.6
.

For
f\in OS\!ym_{n-1}
,
m\geq 0

and
k=1,\dots,n
, we have that

\tau_{k-1}\cdots\tau_{1}x_{1}^{m+k-1}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{1}(f)=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\operatorname{sh}_{i}\big(h_{m+i}^{(k-i)}\big)\tau_{i}\cdots\tau_{1}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{1}(f),

equality in the
ONH_{n}
-supermodule
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
.

Proof.

We prove this by induction on
k
, the case
k=1
being trivial.
For the induction step, we have by induction that

\tau_{k-1}\cdots\tau_{1}x_{1}^{m+k}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{1}(f)=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\operatorname{sh}_{i}\big(h_{m+i+1}^{(k-i)}\big)\tau_{i}\cdots\tau_{1}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{1}(f).

Applying
\tau_{k}
to both sides, we deduce that

\tau_{k}\cdots\tau_{1}x_{1}^{m+k}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{1}(f)=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\left(\tau_{k}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{i}\big(h_{m+i+1}^{(k-i)}\big)\right)\tau_{i}\cdots\tau_{1}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{1}(f)+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}{{}^{s_{k}}}\operatorname{sh}_{i}\big(h_{m+1+i}^{(k-i)}\big)\tau_{k}\tau_{i}\cdots\tau_{1}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{1}(f).

By
eq.

5.10
, we have that
\tau_{k-i}\cdot h_{m+i+1}^{(k-i)}=h_{m+i}^{(k-i+1)}
, so the
i
th term in the first summation becomes

\operatorname{sh}_{i}\big(\tau_{k-i}\cdot h_{m+i+1}^{(k-i)}\big)\tau_{i}\cdots\tau_{1}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{1}(f)=\operatorname{sh}_{i}\big(h_{m+i}^{(k+1-i)}\big)\tau_{i}\cdots\tau_{1}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{1}(f).

The second summation gives zero except when
i=k-1
, when it gives

\operatorname{sh}_{k}\big(h_{m+k}^{(1)}\big)\tau_{k}\cdots\tau_{1}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{1}(f)
.
In total, we obtain the desired
\sum_{i=0}^{k}\operatorname{sh}_{i}\big(h_{m+i}^{(k+1-i)}\big)\tau_{i}\cdots\tau_{1}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{1}(f)
.
∎

Recall for

\lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{n}

that
x^{\lambda}
denotes
x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\lambda_{n}}
.

Theorem 6.7

(
[
E
, Th. 3.8]
)
.

For
\lambda\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{n}}
, we have that

s^{(n)}_{\lambda}=(\omega\xi)_{n}\cdot x^{\lambda}
.

Proof.

The original formula from
[
EKL
]
took the form

(6.11)

s_{\lambda}=(-1)^{\binom{n}{3}}\left[\partial_{w_{0}}\Big(x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\lambda_{n}}x_{1}^{n-1}\cdots x_{n-2}^{2}x_{n-1}\Big)\right]^{w_{0}},

using their notation everywhere.
The result was proved
in
[
E
]
with exactly this in place of our
(\omega\xi)_{n}\cdot x^{\lambda}
.
In
eq.

6.11
, the conjugation by
w_{0}
corresponds up to a sign to an application of our
involution
{\gamma}_{n}
, which commutes with the action of
\partial_{w_{0}}
, again up to a sign,
due to
eqs.

5.17
and
5.19
.
This shows that the right hand side of
eq.

6.11

is equal to
(\omega\xi)_{n}\cdot x^{\lambda}
up to a sign.
Hence,
(\omega\xi)_{n}\cdot x^{\lambda}=\pm s^{(n)}_{\lambda}
.
Presumably, one could see that the sign is actually a plus by carefully keeping track of all of the sign changes in this translation. However, this is rather prone to error, so we give an alternative approach. It suffices by
eq.

6.10

to check that the
x^{\lambda}
-coefficient of

(\omega\xi)_{n}\cdot x^{\lambda}
is 1.
From
eq.

5.18
,
we have that
\omega_{n}=\tau_{n-1}\cdots\tau_{1}\operatorname{sh}_{1}(\omega_{n-1})

and

\xi_{n}=\operatorname{sh}_{1}(\xi_{n-1})x_{1}^{n-1}
.
Also
x^{\lambda}=x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\operatorname{sh}_{1}(x^{\mu})

where
\mu=(\lambda_{2},\dots,\lambda_{n})
.
Using these and induction on
n
, we get that

\displaystyle(\omega\xi)_{n}\cdot x^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\tau_{n-1}\cdots\tau_{1}x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}+n-1}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{1}\big(\omega_{n-1}\xi_{n-1}\cdot x^{\mu}\big)

\displaystyle=\tau_{n-1}\cdots\tau_{1}x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}+n-1}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{1}\big(s_{\mu}^{(n-1)}\big)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\operatorname{sh}_{i}\big(h_{\lambda_{1}+i}^{(n-i)}\big)\tau_{i}\cdots\tau_{1}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{1}\big(s_{\mu}^{(n-1)}\big),

the last equality being an application of
lemma

6.6
.
Now we express this in terms of the monomial basis for
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n}
.
The only place a monomial whose
x_{1}
-exponent is
\geq\lambda_{1}

can arise is from the
i=0
term, which is
h_{\lambda_{1}}^{(n)}\operatorname{sh}_{1}\big(s_{\mu}^{(n-1)}\big)
.
This has leading term exactly
x^{\lambda}
, as required.
∎

Now we are going to discuss a graded superalgebra which may be interpreted as the odd analog of the equivariant cohomology algebra of the Grassmannian.
We set things up initially in greater generality.
Switching our default choice of variable from
n
to
\ell
for reasons that will become clear shortly, suppose that
\alpha\in\Lambda(k,\ell)
. This represents the “shape” of a partial flag variety,
Grassmannians being the special case that
k=2
. Let

(6.12)

OS\!ym_{\alpha}:=\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}\\
i\notin\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{k}\}\end{subarray}}\ker\partial_{i}=\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}\\
i\notin\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{k}\}\end{subarray}}\im\partial_{i},

which is a subalgebra of
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{\ell}
containing
OS\!ym_{\ell}
.
We think of
OS\!ym_{\alpha}
as being the odd analog of the ring of “partial” invariants
\mathbb{F}[x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}]^{S\!_{\alpha}}
. For example, we have that

OS\!ym_{\alpha}=OS\!ym_{\ell}
if
\alpha=(\ell)
,
and
OS\!ym_{\alpha}=O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{\ell}
if
\alpha=(1^{\ell})
.
Note also that the
superalgebra anti-involution
*
of
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{\ell}

leaves
OS\!ym_{\alpha}
invariant, whereas the
involution
{\gamma}_{\ell}
takes
OS\!ym_{\alpha}
to
OS\!ym_{w_{k}(\alpha)}

where
w_{k}(\alpha)=(\alpha_{k},\dots,\alpha_{1})
is the reversed composition.

Consider the following diagram:

(6.13)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces OS\!ym}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overbrace{OS\!ym\otimes\cdots\otimes OS\!ym}^{k\text{ times}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces OS\!ym_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces OS\!ym_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces OS\!ym_{\alpha_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes OS\!ym_{\alpha_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{\alpha_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{\alpha_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{+}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\alpha_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi_{\alpha_{k}}}

The top horizontal map

\Delta^{+}_{k}
is the
(k-1)
th iteration of the comultiplication

\Delta^{+}:OS\!ym\rightarrow OS\!ym\otimes OS\!ym
.
The bottom equality is the canonical identification
explained just after
eq.

2.3
, and the outside square commutes thanks
to
eq.

4.45
.
In view of
corollary

5.5
, the subalgebra
OS\!ym_{\alpha}
of
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{\ell}
is identified with
the subalgebra
OS\!ym_{\alpha_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes OS\!ym_{\alpha_{k}}

of
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{\alpha_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{\alpha_{k}}
.
This shows that the natural inclusion of

OS\!ym_{\ell}
into
OS\!ym_{\alpha}
is induced by the comultiplication
\Delta^{+}
.

Recall
that
w_{\alpha}
is the longest element of
S\!_{\alpha}
and
w^{\alpha}
is the longest element of
[S\!_{\ell}/S\!_{\alpha}]_{\min}
, so that
w_{\ell}=w^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}
.
Noting that
w_{\alpha}=w_{\alpha_{1}}\operatorname{sh}_{\alpha_{1}}\big(w_{\beta}\big)

where
\beta:=(\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{k})
, we recursively define

(6.14)

\displaystyle\omega_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\omega_{\alpha_{1}}\operatorname{sh}_{\alpha_{1}}\big(\omega_{\beta}\big)\>\>(\>=\pm\tau_{w_{\alpha}}),

\displaystyle\xi_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{sh}_{\alpha_{1}}\big(\xi_{\beta}\big)\xi_{\alpha_{1}}.

We get from
lemma

5.1
and induction on
k
that

(6.15)

\omega_{\alpha}\cdot\xi_{\alpha}=1

for any
\alpha
.
The following identity is proved in the same way as
eq.

5.27
:

(6.16)

\omega_{\alpha}\xi_{\alpha}\omega_{\alpha}=\omega_{\alpha}.

Similarly to
eq.

5.28
, it follows that the elements

(6.17)

\displaystyle(\xi\omega)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\xi_{\alpha}\omega_{\alpha},

\displaystyle(\omega\xi)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\omega_{\alpha}\xi_{\alpha}

are primitive idempotents in
ONH_{\alpha}
such that

(6.18)

\displaystyle(\xi\omega)_{\alpha}\cdot O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\xi_{\alpha}OS\!ym_{\alpha},

\displaystyle(\omega\xi)_{\alpha}\cdot O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{\ell}

\displaystyle=OS\!ym_{\alpha}.

Also let

\omega^{\alpha}:=\pm\tau_{w^{\alpha}}
for the particular sign chosen so that

(6.19)

\omega_{\ell}=\omega^{\alpha}\omega_{\alpha}

and let

(6.20)

\xi^{\alpha}:=\omega_{\alpha}\cdot\xi_{\ell}\in OS\!ym_{\alpha}.

We have that

(6.21)

\displaystyle\omega^{\alpha}\cdot\xi^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\xi_{\ell}=\xi_{\alpha}\,\xi^{\alpha}.

The first of these equalities follows because
\omega^{\alpha}\cdot\xi^{\alpha}=\omega^{\alpha}\omega_{\alpha}\cdot\xi_{\ell}=\omega_{\ell}\cdot\xi_{\ell}=1

by
eqs.

6.19
and
5.1
.
To establish the second, one first checks that

\xi_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha}=\pm\xi_{\ell}
for some choice of sign,
and the sign is plus because
\omega_{\ell}\cdot\xi_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha}=\omega^{\alpha}\omega_{\alpha}\cdot\xi_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha}=\omega^{\alpha}\cdot\xi^{\alpha}=1=\omega_{\ell}\cdot\xi_{\ell}
.
Finally, we have that

(6.22)

\omega_{\alpha}\xi_{\alpha}\omega_{\ell}=\omega_{\ell}=\omega_{\ell}\xi_{\alpha}\omega_{\alpha}.

This follows because all three expressions act
in the same way on
\xi_{\ell}\in O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{\ell}
due to
lemmas

5.1
,
6.15
,
6.20
and
6.21
.

Theorem 6.8
.

For
\alpha\in\Lambda(k,\ell)
, the graded superalgebra

OS\!ym_{\alpha}
is free
as a right
OS\!ym_{\ell}
-supermodule
with basis

\big\{p^{(\ell)}_{w}\>|\>w\in[S\!_{\ell}/S\!_{\alpha}]_{\min}\}
.
Each
p^{(\ell)}_{w}
in this basis belongs to
the subalgebra
OS\!ym_{\alpha}\cap O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{\ell-\alpha_{k}}
.

Proof.

By
corollary

4.8
and
eq.

3.8
, we have that

(6.23)

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}OS\!ym_{\alpha}}{\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}OS\!ym_{\ell}}

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{\binom{\ell}{2}}[\ell]^{!}_{q,\pi}}{\prod_{i=1}^{k}q^{\binom{\alpha_{i}}{2}}[\alpha_{i}]^{!}_{q,\pi}}=q^{N(\alpha)}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{\ell}{\alpha}_{q,\pi}=\sum_{w\in[S\!_{n}/S\!_{\alpha}]_{\min}}(\pi q^{2})^{\ell(w)}.

This is the graded rank of a free graded right

OS\!ym_{\ell}
-supermodule with basis

\big\{p^{(\ell)}_{w}\>|\>w\in[S\!_{\ell}/S\!_{\alpha}]_{\min}\}
.
So, to prove the theorem, it just remains to show that
the elements
p^{(\ell)}_{w}\>\left(w\in[S\!_{\ell}/S\!_{\alpha}]_{\min}\right)

belong to
OS\!ym_{\alpha}\cap O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{\ell-\alpha_{k}}

and are linearly independent over
OS\!ym_{\ell}
.
The linear independence is immediate from
theorem

5.4
.

To show that
p_{w}^{(\ell)}\in OS\!ym_{\alpha}
, we
need to show that
\partial_{i}(p^{(\ell)}_{w})=0

for all
i
such that
s_{i}\in S\!_{\alpha}
.
We have that
w^{-1}w_{\ell}=w_{\alpha}w^{\prime}

for some
w^{\prime}\in[S\!_{\alpha}\backslash S\!_{\ell}]_{\min}
.
So

p_{w}^{(\ell)}=\tau_{w^{-1}w_{\ell}}\cdot\xi_{\ell}=\pm\omega_{\alpha}\tau_{w^{\prime}}\cdot\xi_{\ell}
.
Since
\ell(s_{i}w_{\alpha})<\ell(w_{\alpha})
when

s_{i}\in S\!_{\alpha}
, the relations in
ONH_{\ell}

now imply that
\tau_{i}\cdot p_{w}^{(\ell)}=0
.

To show that
p^{(\ell)}_{w}\in O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{\ell-\alpha_{k}}
,
we again use
w^{-1}w_{\ell}=w_{\alpha}w^{\prime}

to deduce that

\tau_{w^{-1}w_{\ell}}=\pm\operatorname{sh}_{\ell-\alpha_{k}}(\omega_{\alpha_{k}})\tau_{w^{\prime\prime}}
for some
w^{\prime\prime}\in S\!_{\ell}
.
By the argument explained in the last sentence of
remark

5.6
, it follows that

p^{(\ell)}_{w}
is a linear combination of terms of the form

\operatorname{sh}_{\ell-\alpha_{k}}(\omega_{\alpha_{k}})\cdot x^{\kappa}

for
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}

with
0\leq\kappa_{i}\leq\ell-i
for all
i
.
It is now clear that
p^{(\ell)}_{w}\in O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{\ell-\alpha_{k}}

since
\operatorname{sh}_{\ell-\alpha_{k}}(\omega_{\alpha_{k}})\cdot x_{\ell}^{\kappa_{\ell}}\cdots x_{\ell-\alpha_{k}+1}^{\kappa_{\ell-\alpha_{k}+1}}
is a scalar by
lemma

5.1
and
degree considerations.
∎

Corollary 6.9
.

Suppose that
\alpha\in\Lambda(k,\ell)
for
k\geq 1
.

(1)

The graded superalgebra
OS\!ym_{\alpha}
is a free as a graded right
OS\!ym_{(\alpha_{1},\ell-\alpha_{1})}
-supermodule
with basis
\Big\{\operatorname{sh}_{\alpha_{1}}(p_{w}^{(\ell-\alpha_{1})})\>\Big|\>w\in(S\!_{\ell-\alpha_{1}}/S\!_{(\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{k})})_{\operatorname{min}}\Big\}
.

(2)

The graded superalgebra
OS\!ym_{\alpha}
is free as a graded right
OS\!ym_{(\ell-\alpha_{k},\alpha_{k})}
-supermodule
with basis
\Big\{{\gamma}_{\ell-\alpha_{k}}\big(p_{w}^{(\ell-\alpha_{k})}\big)\>\Big|\>w\in(S\!_{\ell-\alpha_{k}}/S\!_{(\alpha_{k-1},\dots,\alpha_{1})})_{\operatorname{min}}\Big\}
.

All vectors in the bases described in (1)–(2) belong to the subalgebra

\operatorname{sh}_{\alpha_{1}}\big(OS\!ym_{(\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{k-1})}\big)
.

Proof.

(1) This follows immediately from the theorem.

(2) This follows by applying the involution
{\gamma}_{\ell}

to the the result from (1) with
\alpha
replaced by
the reverse composition
\alpha^{\mathtt{r}}
.
∎

Continuing with
\alpha\in\Lambda(k,\ell)
, we need a few more pieces of notation. For
i=1,\dots,k
, we
define

(6.24)

\displaystyle h_{r}^{(\alpha;i)}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{sh}_{\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{i-1}}\big(h_{r}^{(\alpha_{i})}\big),

\displaystyle e_{r}^{(\alpha;i)}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{sh}_{\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{i-1}}\big(e_{r}^{(\alpha_{i})}\big).

Under the identication of
OS\!ym_{\alpha}

with
OS\!ym_{\alpha_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes OS\!ym_{\alpha_{k}}
from
eq.

6.13
, these
are

1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes h_{r}^{(\alpha_{i})}\otimes 1^{\otimes(k-i)}

and

1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes e_{r}^{(\alpha_{i})}\otimes 1^{\otimes(k-i)}
, respectively.
We use similar notation for other elements of
OS\!ym_{\alpha}

such as
e_{\lambda}^{(\alpha;i)}
,
h_{\lambda}^{(\alpha;i)}
and
s_{\lambda}^{(\alpha;i)}

for
\lambda\in{\Lambda^{\!+}}
.
From
eqs.

4.43
and
4.44
, we get that

(6.25)

\displaystyle e^{(\ell)}_{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1},\dots,r_{k}\geq 0\\
r_{1}+\cdots+r_{k}=r\end{subarray}}e^{(\alpha;1)}_{r_{1}}\cdots e^{(\alpha;k)}_{r_{k}},

\displaystyle h^{(\ell)}_{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1},\dots,r_{k}\geq 0\\
r_{1}+\cdots+r_{k}=r\end{subarray}}h^{(\alpha;k)}_{r_{k}}\cdots h^{(\alpha;1)}_{r_{1}}.

These are more convenient when written in terms of
the generating functions

(6.26)

\displaystyle e^{(\alpha;i)}(t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{r=0}^{\alpha_{i}}(-1)^{r}e^{(\alpha;i)}_{r}t^{\alpha_{i}-r},

\displaystyle h^{(\alpha;i)}(t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{r\geq 0}h^{(\alpha;i)}_{r}t^{-\alpha_{i}-r}.

Now the identities
eq.

6.25
become

(6.27)

\displaystyle e^{(\ell)}(t)

\displaystyle:=e^{(\alpha;1)}(t)e^{(\alpha;2)}(t)\cdots e^{(\alpha;k)}(t),

\displaystyle h^{(\ell)}(t)

\displaystyle:=h^{(\alpha;k)}(t)\cdots h^{(\alpha;2)}(t)h^{(\alpha;1)}(t).

These identities, which generalize
eqs.

4.55
and
4.56
, together with the infinite Grassmannian relation
eq.

4.57

are useful when moving between different families of generators,
as illustrated by the following lemma.

Lemma 6.10
.

Suppose that
\ell=n+n^{\prime}
and
r\geq 0
.

(1)

We have that

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{r}(-1)^{s}h_{r-s}^{(n)}e_{s}^{(\ell)}=(-1)^{r}\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big(e_{r}^{(n^{\prime})}\big)
,
which is zero for
r>n^{\prime}
.

(2)

We have that

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{r}(-1)^{s}e_{s}^{(\ell)}\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big(h_{r-s}^{(n^{\prime})}\big)=(-1)^{r}e_{r}^{(n)}
,
which is zero for
r>n
.

Proof.

(1)
The first identity
from
eq.

6.27
when
\alpha=(n,n^{\prime})
plus
eq.

4.57

gives that

\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big(e^{(n^{\prime})}(t)\big)=h^{(n)}(t)e^{(\ell)}(t).

Now
equate the coefficients of
t^{n^{\prime}-r}
on both sides.

(2)
Similar.
∎

Lemma 6.11
.

Suppose that
n\geq 0
.
The following hold in
O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{n+1}
for any
m\geq 0
:

(1)

\displaystyle x_{n+1}^{m+n+1}=-\sum_{q=0}^{n}\left(\sum_{r=0}^{m}(-1)^{m+n+1-q-r}h_{r}^{(n+1)}e_{m+n+1-q-r}^{(n+1)}\right)x_{n+1}^{q}
;

(2)

\displaystyle x_{1}^{m+n+1}=-\sum_{p=0}^{n}x_{1}^{p}\left(\sum_{s=0}^{m}(-1)^{m+n+1-p-s}e^{(n+1)}_{m+n+1-p-s}h_{s}^{(n+1)}\right).

Proof.

(1)
Induction on
m
.
The base case
m=0
follows as
\sum_{q=0}^{n+1}(-1)^{n+1-q}e_{n+1-q}^{(n+1)}x_{n+1}^{q}=0

due to
lemma

6.10
(2) with
\ell,n,n^{\prime}
and
r
replaced with
n+1,n,1
and
n+1
,
respectively.
Now take the identity we are trying to prove for some
m\geq 0

and multiply on the right by
x_{n+1}
to obtain

(6.28)

x_{n}^{m+1+n+1}=-\sum_{q=0}^{n}\left(\sum_{r=0}^{m}(-1)^{m+n+1-q-r}h_{r}^{(n+1)}e_{m+n+1-q-r}^{(n+1)}\right)x_{n+1}^{q+1}.

The
q=n
term of the summation here is

-\sum_{r=0}^{m}(-1)^{m+1-r}h_{r}^{(n+1)}e_{m+1-r}^{(n+1)}x_{n+1}^{n+1}
, which
equals
h_{m+1}^{(n+1)}x_{n+1}^{n+1}
by the infinite Grassmannian relation

eq.

4.11
. Using the
m=0
case of the identity we are proving
this can then be rewritten as

-\sum_{q=0}^{n}(-1)^{n+1-q}h_{m+1}^{(n+1)}e_{n+1-q}^{(n+1)}x_{n+1}^{q}.

For the terms of
eq.

6.28
with
0\leq q\leq n-1
, we reindex the summation
replacing
q
by
q-1
to obtain

-\sum_{q=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{r=0}^{m}(-1)^{m+1+n+1-q-r}h_{r}^{(n+1)}e_{m+1+n+1-q-r}^{(n+1)}\right)x_{n+1}^{q}.

The expression in brackets is zero for
q=0
since

e_{m+1+n+1-r}^{(n+1)}=0
for all
0\leq r\leq m
, so we can sum instead from
q=0
to
n
.
Thus, we have shown that

\displaystyle x_{n+1}^{m+1+n+1}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{q=0}^{n}(-1)^{n+1-q}h_{m+1}^{(n+1)}e_{n+1-q}^{(n+1)}x_{n+1}^{q}-\sum_{q=0}^{n}\left(\sum_{r=0}^{m}(-1)^{m+1+n+1-q-r}h_{r}^{(n+1)}e_{m+1+n+1-q-r}^{(n+1)}\right)x_{n+1}^{q}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{q=0}^{n}\left((-1)^{n+1-q}h_{m+1}^{(n+1)}e_{n+1-q}^{(n+1)}+\sum_{r=0}^{m}(-1)^{m+1+n+1-q-r}h_{r}^{(n+1)}e_{m+1+n+1-q-r}^{(n+1)}\right)x_{n+1}^{q}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{q=1}^{n+1}\left(\sum_{r=0}^{m+1}(-1)^{m+1+n+1-q-r}h_{r}^{(n+1)}e_{m+1+n+1-q-r}^{(n+1)}\right)x_{n+1}^{q}.

This is just what is needed for the induction step.

(2)
This is similar, or may be proved by applying
{\gamma}_{n+1}\circ*
to (1).
∎

Now we focus on the most important case
k=2
, so

\alpha=(n,n^{\prime})\in\Lambda(2,\ell)
for some
n,n^{\prime}\geq 0
.
Then
\xi_{\alpha}=\xi_{(n,n^{\prime})}=\operatorname{sh}_{n}(\xi_{n^{\prime}})\xi_{n}
.

Theorem 6.12
.

Suppose that
\ell=n+n^{\prime}
for
n,n^{\prime}\geq 0
.
Then
OS\!ym_{(n,n^{\prime})}
has the following two bases as a free graded
right
OS\!ym_{\ell}
-supermodule:

(1)

\left\{s^{(n)}_{\lambda}\>\big|\>\lambda\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{n\times n^{\prime}}}\right\}
;

(2)

\left\{\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big(\sigma^{(n^{\prime})}_{\mu}\big)\>\big|\>\mu\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{n^{\prime}\times n}}\right\}
.

Also let
\Tr:OS\!ym_{(n,n^{\prime})}\rightarrow OS\!ym_{\ell}
be the linear map

a\mapsto\omega_{\ell}\>\xi_{(n,n^{\prime})}\cdot a
.
This map is a homogeneous homomorphism of graded right
OS\!ym_{\ell}
-supermodules
of degree
-2nn^{\prime}
and parity
nn^{\prime}\pmod{2}
,
and the bases (1)–(2) satisfy

(6.29)

\Tr\left(s^{(n)}_{\lambda}\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big(\sigma^{(n^{\prime})}_{\mu}\big)\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\operatorname{sgn}(\mu)&\text{if $\mu^{\mathtt{t}}_{i}=n^{\prime}-\lambda_{n+1-i}\text{ for }i=1,\dots,n$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

for
\operatorname{sgn}(\mu)\in\{\pm 1\}
with
\operatorname{sgn}(\varnothing)=1
; see
corollary

7.6
below for a formula for
\operatorname{sgn}(\mu)
for general
\mu
.

Proof.

The main work here is to prove
eq.

6.29
.
This turns out to be significantly harder than the analogous
formula in the ordinary even theory;
see
[
EKL
, Rem. 4.12]
for an illuminating example.
Fortunately, the details are already worked out in

[
EKL
, Prop. 4.11]
up to an undetermined
sign since our conventions are different.
To keep track of this sign, we repeat
the first few steps of the proof in
[
EKL
]
in our set up.
By
theorems

6.7
,
5.19
,
5.17
,
6.8
and
6.22
, we have that

\displaystyle\Tr\left(s^{(n)}_{\lambda}\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big(\sigma^{(n^{\prime})}_{\mu}\big)\right)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\ell}\;\xi_{(n,n^{\prime})}\cdot s^{(n)}_{\lambda}\operatorname{sh}_{n}\Big({\gamma}_{n^{\prime}}\big(s^{(n^{\prime})}_{\mu}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\ell}\;\xi_{(n,n^{\prime})}\cdot\big(\omega_{n}\xi_{n}\cdot x^{\lambda}\big)\;\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big({\gamma}_{n^{\prime}}(\omega_{n^{\prime}}\xi_{n^{\prime}}\cdot x^{\mu})\big)

\displaystyle=\zeta_{n^{\prime}}\omega_{\ell}\;\xi_{(n,n^{\prime})}\omega_{(n,n^{\prime})}\cdot\;\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big({\gamma}_{n^{\prime}}(\xi_{n^{\prime}})\big)\xi_{n}x^{\lambda}\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big({\gamma}_{n^{\prime}}(x^{\mu})\big)

(6.30)

\displaystyle=\zeta_{n^{\prime}}\omega_{\ell}\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big({\gamma}_{n^{\prime}}(\xi_{n^{\prime}})\big)\xi_{n}x^{\lambda}\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big({\gamma}_{n^{\prime}}(x^{\mu})\big).

Up to another sign, the monomial appearing after the
\cdot
in
eq.

6.30
is as
considered in
[
EKL
, Lem. 4.9]
, so applying that lemma gives
that
\Tr\left(s^{(n)}_{\lambda}\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big(\sigma^{({n^{\prime}})}_{\mu}\big)\right)

is
\pm 1
if
\mu^{\mathtt{t}}_{i}=n^{\prime}-\lambda_{n+1-i}\text{ for }i=1,\dots,n
,
and it is zero otherwise.
It remains to check that
eq.

6.30
equals
+1
in the special case that

\lambda=({n^{\prime}}^{n})
and

\mu=\varnothing
.
To see this, one first checks that

\xi_{n}x_{1}^{n^{\prime}}\cdots x_{n}^{n^{\prime}}=x_{n}^{n^{\prime}}x_{n-1}^{n^{\prime}+1}\cdots x_{1}^{n+n^{\prime}-1}
.
Hence,
letting
\omega_{\ell}=\tau\operatorname{sh}_{n}(\omega_{n^{\prime}})
for

\tau\in ONH_{\ell}
,

eq.

6.30
simplifies in this case to give

\displaystyle\Tr\left(s^{(n)}_{({n^{\prime}}^{n})}\right)

\displaystyle=\zeta_{n^{\prime}}\tau\operatorname{sh}_{n}(\omega_{n^{\prime}})\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big({\gamma}_{n^{\prime}}(\xi_{n^{\prime}})\big)x_{n}^{n^{\prime}}x_{n-1}^{n^{\prime}+1}\cdots x_{1}^{n+n^{\prime}-1}=\tau\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big({\gamma}_{n^{\prime}}(\omega_{n^{\prime}}\cdot\xi_{n^{\prime}})\big)\cdot x_{n}^{n^{\prime}}x_{n-1}^{n^{\prime}+1}\cdots x_{1}^{n^{\prime}+n-1}

\displaystyle=\tau\cdot x_{n}^{n^{\prime}}x_{n-1}^{n^{\prime}+1}\cdots x_{1}^{n+n^{\prime}-1}=\tau\operatorname{sh}_{n}(\omega_{n^{\prime}})\cdot\operatorname{sh}_{n}(\xi_{n^{\prime}})x_{n}^{n^{\prime}}x_{n-1}^{n^{\prime}+1}\cdots x_{1}^{n+n^{\prime}-1}=\omega_{\ell}\cdot\xi_{\ell}=1.

Now
eq.

6.29
is proved.

It is clear from the definition that
\Tr
is a homogeneous
homomorphism of graded right
OS\!ym_{\ell}
-supermodules
of degree
-2nn^{\prime}
and parity
nn^{\prime}\pmod{2}
.
It remains to show that the elements (1) and (2) are bases.
To see that the elements (1) are linearly independent over
OS\!ym_{\ell}
,
take a linear relation
\sum_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}^{(n)}a_{\lambda}
for

a_{\lambda}\in OS\!ym_{\ell}
. To see that
a_{\lambda}=0
for any given
\lambda
,
let
\mu\in{\Lambda^{\!+}}
be defined so that

\mu^{\mathtt{t}}_{i}=n^{\prime}-\lambda_{n+1-i}
for
i=1,\dots,n

and
\mu^{\mathtt{t}}_{i}=0
for
i>n
.
Then we have using
eq.

6.29
that

0=\Tr\bigg(\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big(\sigma_{\mu}^{(n^{\prime})}\big)\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}}s_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(n)}a_{\lambda^{\prime}}\bigg)=\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}}(-1)^{|\lambda^{\prime}||\mu|}\Tr\Big(s_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(n)}\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big(\sigma_{\mu}^{(n^{\prime})}\big)\Big)a_{\lambda^{\prime}}=(-1)^{|\lambda||\mu|}\operatorname{sgn}(\mu)a_{\lambda}.

This establishes the linear independence.
As
s_{\lambda}^{(n)}
is of degree
2|\lambda|
and parity
|\lambda|\pmod{2}
,
we deduce from
corollary

3.2
that the elements (1) generate
a free graded right
OS\!ym_{\ell}
-supermodule of graded rank

q^{nn^{\prime}}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{\ell}{n}_{q,\pi}
.
In view of
theorem

6.8
, it follows that this submodule is
all of
OS\!ym_{(n,n^{\prime})}
.
This proves that (1) is a basis.
A similar argument gives that (2) is a basis too.
∎

Corollary 6.13
.

Suppose that
\ell=n+d+n^{\prime}
for
n,d,n^{\prime}\geq 0
.
Then

OS\!ym_{(n,d,n^{\prime})}
is a free right
OS\!ym_{(n,n^{\prime}+d)}
-supermodule
with basis

\Big\{\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big(s_{\lambda}^{(d)}\big)\>\Big|\>\lambda\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{d\times n^{\prime}}}\Big\}
,
and it is a free right
OS\!ym_{(n+d,n^{\prime})}
-supermodule
with basis
\Big\{\operatorname{sh}_{n}\big(\sigma_{\lambda}^{(d)}\big)\>\Big|\>\lambda\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{d\times n}}\Big\}
.

7.
The odd analog of cohomology of Grassmannians

Continue with
\ell=n+n^{\prime}
.
In the purely even theory, when all of the algebras involved are commutative,
the analog of the map
\Tr
from
theorem

6.12
is actually a graded bimodule
homomorphism, so that it gives a trace making
S\!ym_{(n,n^{\prime})}

into a graded Frobenius algebra over
S\!ym_{\ell}
.
However, in the odd case,
OS\!ym_{(n,n^{\prime})}
is usually

not
a Frobenius extension of
OS\!ym_{\ell}
,
e.g., it is already false in the case
n=n^{\prime}=1
since
one can check directly that

OS\!ym_{2}<O\hskip-0.42677ptPol_{2}
has no complement as a graded
(OS\!ym_{2},OS\!ym_{2})
-superbimodule.
This is a significant obstruction to the development of the odd theory.
At this point in
[
EKL
, Sec. 5]
, the obstruction is avoided by passing
to the finite-dimensional graded superalgebra

(7.1)

\overline{OH}_{n}^{\ell}:=OS\!ym_{n}\>\big/\>\big\langle h_{r}^{(n)}\>\big|\>r>n^{\prime}\big\rangle\>\,\left(\>=OS\!ym\>\big/\>\big\langle h_{r},e_{s}\>\big|\>r>n^{\prime},s>n\big\rangle\;\right).

This is called the
odd Grassmannian cohomology algebra

since it is an odd analog of the cohomology algebra
H^{*}\big(\operatorname{Gr}_{n}^{\ell};\mathbb{F}\big)

of the Grassmannian
\operatorname{Gr}_{n}^{\ell}
of
n
-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{C}^{\ell}
.

We denote the image of
a\in OS\!ym_{n}
in
\overline{OH}_{n}^{\ell}
by
\bar{a}
.
The first part of following theorem is

[
EKL
, Prop. 5.4]
, but we give a different argument which gives
extra information.

Theorem 7.1
.

Suppose that
\ell=n+n^{\prime}
.
The odd Schur polynomials

\bar{s}_{\lambda}^{(n)}
for

\lambda\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{n\times n^{\prime}}}

give a linear basis for
\overline{OH}_{n}^{\ell}\,
,
and all other
\bar{s}_{\lambda}^{(n)}
are zero.
Moreover, viewing
\mathbb{F}
as
a graded
OS\!ym_{\ell}
-supermodule in the obvious way,
there is a commuting diagram

(7.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{OH}^{\ell}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces OS\!ym_{(n,n^{\prime})}\otimes_{OS\!ym_{\ell}}\mathbb{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{OH}^{\ell}_{n^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}\otimes_{OS\!ym_{\ell}}OS\!ym_{(n,n^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{a}\mapsto a\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\psi}_{n}^{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{a}\mapsto(-1)^{n\operatorname{par}(a)}1\otimes\operatorname{sh}_{n}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes a\mapsto a^{*}\otimes 1}

of isomorphisms in which

(1)

the top map is an even degree 0 isomorphism
of graded left
OS\!ym_{n}
-supermodules;

(2)

the bottom map is an even degree 0
isomorphism
of graded right
OS\!ym_{n^{\prime}}
-supermodules
for the action
on

\mathbb{F}\otimes_{OS\!ym_{\ell}}OS\!ym_{(n,n^{\prime})}

defined by restriction along
\operatorname{sh}_{n}\circ\operatorname{p}^{n}:OS\!ym_{n^{\prime}}\rightarrow OS\!ym_{(n,n^{\prime})}
;

(3)

the right hand map is an even linear isomorphism of degree 0;

(4)

the left hand map
\overline{\psi}_{n}^{\ell}
is the graded superalgebra isomorphism induced
by the involution
\psi\circ\operatorname{p}^{n}
of
OS\!ym
.

Remark 7.2
.

The inclusion of the parity involution
\operatorname{p}^{n}

in the definition of the left and bottom maps in
eq.

7.2
is
hard to justify at this point—it could simply be omitted in both places
and the simplified result is also true. The signs have been included for consistency with
theorem

8.5
below, in
which they are essential.

Proof of
theorem

7.1
.

(1)
To construct the top map so that it is a homomorphism
of graded left
OS\!ym_{n}
-supermodules, we must show that

OS\!ym_{(n,n^{\prime})}\otimes_{OS\!ym_{\ell}}\mathbb{F}
can be made into a
graded left
\overline{OH}^{\ell}_{n}
-supermodule
so that
\bar{a}\cdot(b\otimes 1)=ab\otimes 1
for all
a\in OS\!ym_{n},b\in OS\!ym_{(n,n^{\prime})}
.
Since it is already a graded left
OS\!ym_{n}
-supermodule,
and
\overline{OH}^{\ell}_{n}
is the quotient of
OS\!ym_{n}

by the relations
h_{r}^{(n)}=0
for
r>n^{\prime}
,
it suffices to check that

h_{r}^{(n)}
acts as zero on
OS\!ym_{(n,n^{\prime})}\otimes_{OS\!ym_{\ell}}\mathbb{F}

for all
r>n^{\prime}
.
By
theorem

6.12
(2), any homogeneous element of

OS\!ym_{(n,n^{\prime})}\otimes_{OS\!ym_{\ell}}\mathbb{F}
can be written as

\operatorname{sh}_{n}(b)\otimes 1
for
b\in OS\!ym_{n^{\prime}}
.
Now we must show that

h_{r}^{(n)}\operatorname{sh}_{n}(b)\otimes 1=0
for
r>n^{\prime}
.
This follows from the calculation

\displaystyle h_{r}^{(n)}\operatorname{sh}_{n}(b)\otimes 1
