Theorem 12.1
.

Under the identification
eq.

12.1
of

\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\ell}K_{0}(OH_{n}^{\ell})

with

\mathbf{V}(-\ell)
,
the

\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]^{\pi}
-module endomorphisms induced by
tensoring with odd Grassmannian bimodules
correspond
to endomorphisms defined
by actions of elements of
\mathbf{U}_{q,\pi}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
according to the following dictionary:

(1)

\big[U_{n}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n}^{\ell}}-\big]\equiv q^{n}E1_{2n-\ell}

and, more generally,
\big[U_{(d);n}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n}^{\ell}}-\big]\equiv q^{nd}E^{(d)}1_{2n-\ell}
;

(2)

\big[V_{n}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n+1}^{\ell}}-\big]\equiv q^{\ell-3n-1}1_{2n-\ell}F

and
\big[V_{n;(d)}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n+d}^{\ell}}-\big]\equiv q^{d(\ell-3n-2d+1)}1_{2n-\ell}F^{(d)}
.

Also, for
-\ell\leq k\leq\ell

with
k\equiv\ell\pmod{2}
,
the map
T:1_{-k}\mathbf{V}(-\ell)\rightarrow 1_{k}\mathbf{V}(-\ell)

from
theorem

3.6

corresponds to the
\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]^{\pi}
-module
homomorphism

(12.2)

\displaystyle T:K_{0}(OH_{n}^{\ell})

\displaystyle\rightarrow K_{0}(OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}),

\displaystyle\big[OH_{n}^{\ell}\big]

\displaystyle\mapsto(-1)^{n}(\pi q^{2})^{\binom{n+1}{2}+nk}q^{-nk}\big[OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}\big]

where
n:=\frac{\ell-k}{2}
and
n^{\prime}:=\frac{\ell+k}{2}
.

Proof.

(1)
Note here we are assuming implicitly that
0\leq n\leq\ell-1

and

0\leq n\leq\ell-d
, respectively
so that
U_{n}^{\ell}
and
U_{(d);n}^{\ell}

are defined.
By
lemma

10.8
(2b), we have that

\big[U_{n}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n}^{\ell}}OH_{n}^{\ell}\big]=q^{n}[n+1]_{q,\pi}\big[OH_{n+1}^{\ell}\big].

Also
Eb_{n}^{\ell}=[n+1]_{q,\pi}b_{n}^{\ell}
.
It follows that

\big[U_{n}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n}^{\ell}}-\big]
and

q^{n}E1_{2n-\ell}

define the same endomorphisms of
\mathbf{V}(-\ell)
.
For the more general assertion, take
d\geq 1
.
By
lemma

10.5
(2) and
eq.

3.3
, we have that

\big[U_{(1^{d});n}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n}^{\ell}}-\big]=q^{\binom{d}{2}}[d]^{!}_{q,\pi}\big[U_{(d);n}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n}^{\ell}}-\big]
.
Also
U_{(1^{d});n}^{\ell}\simeq U^{\ell}_{n+d-1}\otimes_{OH_{n+d-1}^{\ell}}\cdots\otimes_{OH_{n+1}^{\ell}}U_{n}^{\ell}

by
eq.

10.18
,
so we deduce using the special case already treated that

\displaystyle q^{\binom{d}{2}}[d]^{!}_{q,\pi}\big[U_{(d);n}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n}^{\ell}}-\big]

\displaystyle=q^{nd+\binom{d}{2}}E^{d}1_{2n-\ell}=q^{nd+\binom{d}{2}}[d]^{!}_{q,\pi}E^{(d)}1_{2n-\ell}.

Cancelling
q^{\binom{d}{2}}[d]^{!}_{q,\pi}

gives the required conclusion.

(2) Again we are assuming that

0\leq n\leq\ell-1
and
0\leq n\leq\ell-d
, respectively.
The first step is to show that

\big[\widetilde{V}_{n}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n+1}^{\ell}}-\big]=q^{\ell-n-1}\pi^{n}1_{2n-\ell}F
,
which follows from
lemma

10.8
(1a) like in the proof of (1).
Hence, since
V_{n}^{\ell}=(\Pi Q^{-2})^{n}\widetilde{V}_{n}^{\ell}
,
we get that

\big[V_{n}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n+1}^{\ell}}-\big]=q^{\ell-3n-1}1_{2n-\ell}F
.
The passage from this to
the more general
result about
V_{n;(d)}^{\ell}

follows in a similar way to the argument given in (1).

Now consider the final statement about
T
.
Take
k
and
n=\frac{\ell-k}{2},n^{\prime}=\frac{\ell+k}{2}

as in the statement of the theorem.
We saw in
eq.

3.20
that
T(b_{n}^{\ell})=(-1)^{n}\pi^{\binom{n}{2}+nn^{\prime}}q^{n+nn^{\prime}}b_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}
.
Using the identification
eq.

12.1
,
it follows that

T\Big(\big[OH_{n}^{\ell}\big]\Big)=(-1)^{n}\pi^{\binom{n}{2}+nn^{\prime}}q^{n+nn^{\prime}}\big[OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}\big].

On replacing
n^{\prime}
by
k+n
,
this becomes the formula in the statement of the theorem.
∎

The goal in the remainder section is to
categorify
T:1_{-k}\mathbf{V}(-\ell)\rightarrow 1_{k}\mathbf{V}(-\ell)

for all
-\ell\leq k\leq\ell
with
k\equiv\ell\pmod{2}
.
Throughout, we let
n:=\frac{\ell-k}{2}
and
n^{\prime}:=\frac{\ell+k}{2}=n+k

so that
2n-\ell=-k
and
2n^{\prime}-\ell=k
.
Since
n+n^{\prime}=\ell
,
theorem

8.5
(4) shows that
the graded superalgebras

OH_{n}^{\ell}
and
OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}
are isomorphic.

Definition 12.2
.

For
0\leq d\leq n
, let

\displaystyle C_{d}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}U^{\ell}_{(k+d);n-d}\otimes_{OH_{n-d}^{\ell}}V_{n-d;(d)}^{\ell}&\text{if $d\geq-k$,}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

The
singular Rouquier complex
for odd Grassmannian bimodules
is
the following sequence of graded

\big(OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell},OH_{n}^{\ell}\big)
-superbimodules
and even degree 0 superbimodule homomorphisms
in
\mathpzc{OGBim}_{\ell}
:

(12.5)

0\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}C_{n}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\cdots\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}C_{d}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}C_{d-1}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\cdots\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}C_{1}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}C_{0}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}0

where
\partial_{d}=0
unless
\max(0,-k)<d\leq n
, in which case

\partial_{d}:C_{d}\rightarrow C_{d-1}
is the even degree 0
superbimodule homomorphism defined by the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\ell}_{(k+d);n-d}\otimes_{OH_{n-d}^{\ell}}V_{n-d;(d)}^{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\ell}_{(k+d-1,1);n-d}\otimes_{OH_{n-d}^{\ell}}V_{n-d;(1,d-1)}^{\ell}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{inc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{(k+d-1),(1)}^{\prime}\otimes\;c_{(1),(d-1)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\ell}_{(k+d-1);n-d+1}\otimes_{OH_{n-d+1}^{\ell}}U^{\ell}_{n-d}\otimes_{OH_{n-d}^{\ell}}V_{n-d}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n-d+1}^{\ell}}V_{n-d+1;(d-1)}^{\ell}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\otimes\operatorname{ev}_{n-d}\otimes\id}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\ell}_{(k+d-1);n-d+1}\otimes_{OH_{n-d+1}^{\ell}}OH_{n-d+1}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n-d+1}^{\ell}}V_{n-d+1;(d-1)}^{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\ell}_{(k+d-1);n-d+1}\otimes_{OH_{n-d+1}^{\ell}}V_{n-d+1;(d-1)}^{\ell}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{can}}

Theorem 12.3
.

The singular Rouquier complex
eq.

12.5
is a chain complex
with homology that is zero in all except for the top (
n
th) homological degree.
Moreover,
as a graded

\big(OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell},OH_{n}^{\ell})
-superbimodule
the top homology is
\simeq(\Pi Q^{2})^{\binom{n+1}{2}+nk}OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}

viewed as a graded left
OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}
-supermodule by the natural action
and as a graded right
OH_{n}^{\ell}
-supermodule
by restricting the natural right action of
OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}
along
some graded superalgebra isomorphism

OH_{n}^{\ell}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits}}OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}
.

The following corollary follows immediately. To formulate it, let

K^{b}(OH_{n}^{\ell}{\operatorname{-pgsmod}})
be the bounded homotopy supercategory
of the graded supercategory finitely generated projective
graded left
OH_{n}^{\ell}
-supermodules;
in the definition of this
we require that differentials and homotopies are even of degree 0 but
chain maps between cochain complexes can be constructed using
arbitrary morphisms in
OH_{n}^{\ell}{\operatorname{-pgsmod}}
.
By Euler characteristic,
the Grothendieck group of the underlying ordinary category
is identified with
K_{0}(OH_{n}^{\ell})
, hence,
via
eq.

12.1
, with
1_{-k}\mathbf{V}(-\ell)
.

Corollary 12.4
.

Tensoring with the singular Rouquier complex

eq.

12.5
(viewed now as a cochain complex rather than a chain complex
as usual when working with homotopy categories)
then taking the total complex
defines a graded superequivalence

K^{b}(OH_{n}^{\ell}{\operatorname{-pgsmod}})\rightarrow K^{b}(OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}{\operatorname{-pgsmod}})
.
The induced
\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]^{\pi}
-module isomorphism

1_{-k}\mathbf{V}(-\ell)\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits}}1_{k}\mathbf{V}(-\ell)

at the level of Grothendieck groups
is equal to
q^{nk}T
for
T
as in
eq.

12.2
.

The remainder of the section is devoted to the proof of
theorem

12.3
, which will be carried out with a series of lemmas.
We assume for simplicity of notation that
k\geq 0
, although with obvious modifications the arguments work for negative
k
too.

Lemma 12.5
.

We have that
\partial_{d-1}\circ\partial_{d}=0
for
d=1,\dots,n+1
,
hence,
eq.

12.5
is a chain complex.

Proof.

By the super interchange law,
\partial_{d-1}\circ\partial_{d}

factorizes as the composition first of the embedding

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{d}=U_{(k+d);n-d}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n-d}^{\ell}}V_{n-d;(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{(k+d-2,2);n-d}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n-d}^{\ell}}V_{n-d;(2,d-2)}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{inc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{(k+d-2),(2)}\otimes\;c_{(2),(d-2)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\qquad\qquad\qquad U_{(k+d-2);n-d+2}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n-d+2}^{\ell}}U_{(2);n-d}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n-d}^{\ell}}V^{\ell}_{n-d;(2)}\otimes_{OH_{n-d+2}^{\ell}}V^{\ell}_{n-d+2;(d-2)}}

then the map
\id\otimes(\partial\circ\iota)\otimes\id

from there to

C_{d-2}=U_{(k+d-2);n-d+2}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n-d+2}^{\ell}}V^{\ell}_{n-d+2;(d-2)}
,
where

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota:U_{(2);n-d}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n-d}^{\ell}}V_{n-d;(2)}^{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{(1,1);n-d}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n-d}^{\ell}}V_{n-d;(1,1)}^{\ell}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{inc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{(1),(1)}\otimes\;c_{(1),(1)}}

\phantom{oiergnoweirjgf}U_{n-d+1}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n-d+1}^{\ell}}U_{n-d}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n-d}^{\ell}}V_{n-d}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n-d+1}^{\ell}}V_{n-d+1}^{\ell},

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial:\!U_{n-d+1}^{\ell}\!\otimes_{OH_{n-d+1}^{\ell}}\!\!\!\!U_{n-d}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n-d}^{\ell}}\!\!\!V_{n-d}^{\ell}\!\otimes_{OH_{n-d+1}^{\ell}}\!\!\!V_{n-d+1}^{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{n-d+1}^{\ell}\!\otimes_{OH_{n-d+1}^{\ell}}\!\!\!\!OH_{n-d+1}^{\ell}\!\otimes_{OH_{n-d+1}^{\ell}}\!\!\!V_{n-d+1}^{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\otimes\operatorname{ev}_{n-d}\otimes\id}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phantom{aoeikrerfefg;rgja;porgi}U_{n-d+1}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n-d+1}^{\ell}}V_{n-d+1}^{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces OH_{n-d+2}^{\ell}.\phantom{rgwtwrthtgrw}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{n-d+1}}

Thus, we are reduced to proving that
\partial
takes vectors in the image
of
\iota
to zero.
By
lemma

10.5
, the image of
\iota

is equal to the image of the projection

\rho_{(1^{2});n-d}\big((\xi\omega)_{2}\big)\otimes\lambda_{n-d;(1^{2})}\big((\omega\xi)_{2}\big)
.
This projection equals

\rho_{(1^{2});n-d}(x_{1}\tau_{1})\otimes\lambda_{n-d;(1^{2})}(\tau_{1}x_{1})=\big(\rho_{(1^{2});n-d}(\tau_{1})\otimes\lambda_{n-d;(1^{2})}(\tau_{1})\big)\circ\big(\rho_{(1^{2});n-d}(x_{1})\otimes\lambda_{n-d;(1^{2})}(x_{1})\big).

Finally, to complete the proof,
we observe that

\partial\circ\big(\rho_{(1^{2});n-d}(\tau_{1})\otimes\lambda_{n-d;(1^{2})}(\tau_{1})\big)=0

because

\partial\circ\big(\rho_{(1^{2});n-d}(\tau_{1})\otimes\id\otimes\id\big)=\partial\circ\big(\id\otimes\id\otimes\lambda_{n-d;(1^{2})}(\tau_{1})\big)

thanks to
lemma

11.10
,
and

\lambda_{n-d;(1^{2})}(\tau_{1})\circ\lambda_{n-d;(1^{2})}(\tau_{1})=\lambda_{n-d;(1^{2})}\big(\tau_{1}^{2}\big)=0
.
∎

Now we need to understand the “numerology” of
eq.

12.5
.
In fact, the combinatorial
lemma

3.3
derived long ago
is just what we need for this. Recall the definitions
of
b_{m,n}(r),c_{m,n}(r)\in\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]^{\pi}

made in the statement of that lemma.
The following shows that
c_{n+k,n}(d)
is
the graded superrank of
C_{d}
either as a
free graded
right
OH_{n}^{d}
-supermodule or a free graded left
OH_{n^{\prime}}^{d}
-supermodule.
We will also see in a bit that
b_{n+k,n}(d)
is the graded rank of

\im\partial_{d}
for
d=0,1,\dots,n
.

Lemma 12.6
.

The vectors

(12.6)

\bigg\{u_{(k+d);n-d}\big(\bar{\sigma}^{(k+d)}_{\lambda}\big)\otimes v_{n-d;(d)}\big(\bar{\sigma}^{(d)}_{\mu}\big)\>\bigg|\>(\lambda,\mu)\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(k+d)\times n}}\times{\Lambda^{\!+}_{d\times(n-d)}}\bigg\}

give a basis for
C_{d}
as a free right
OH_{n}^{\ell}
-supermodule.
Hence,
as a graded right
OH_{n}^{\ell}
-supermodule,

C_{d}
is free
of graded superrank
c_{n+k,n}^{(d)}
.
It is also free as a graded left
OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}
-superbimodule
with the same graded superrank.

Proof.

lemma

10.4
implies that it is free as a graded right
OH_{n}^{\ell}
-supermodule with basis

eq.

12.6
.
The formula for its graded superrank then follows using

corollary

3.2
. It is also free as a graded left
OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}
-supermodule
thanks to
lemma

10.4
again. Since
OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}\cong OH_{n}^{\ell}
, its graded superrank for
OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}

is the same as for
OH_{n}^{\ell}
.
∎

Recall that
OH_{n}^{\ell}
is positively graded
with degree 0 component isomorphic
the ground field
\mathbb{F}
.
We apply the functor

-\otimes_{OH_{n}^{\ell}}\mathbb{F}
to
eq.

12.5
to obtain the chain complex

(12.7)

0\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\overline{C}_{n}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\cdots\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\overline{C}_{d}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\overline{C}_{d-1}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\cdots\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\overline{C}_{1}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\overline{C}_{0}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}0

of graded left
\overline{OH}_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}
-supermodules.

lemma

12.6
implies that

\overline{C}_{d}
is of graded superdimension
c_{n+k,n}(d)
.

Lemma 12.7
.

Suppose we are given that
\dim\im\bar{\partial}_{d}\geq|b_{n+k,n}(d)|

for
d=1,\dots,n
,
where
|b_{n+k,n}(d)|
denotes the natural number obtained by
applying the evaluation
map
\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]^{\pi}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z},q\mapsto 1,\pi\mapsto 1

to
b_{n+k,n}(d)
.
Then
theorem

12.3
is true.

Proof.

We first consider the specialized complex
eq.

12.7
,
showing that
\im\overline{\partial}_{d}=\ker\overline{\partial}_{d-1}

and that it is of graded superdimension
b_{n+k,n}(d)

for each
d=1,\dots,n
. This follows by induction
on
d
, defining
\overline{\partial}_{-1}
to be the zero map
so that we can start the induction at
d=0
. The induction base
holds because
b_{n+k,n}(0)=0
.
For the induction step, take
0\leq d<n
and assume
that
\im\overline{\partial}_{d}=\ker\overline{\partial}_{d-1}

is of graded superdimension
b_{n+k,n}(d)
.
We have that

\overline{C}_{d}=\overline{B}^{\prime}_{d}\oplus\overline{Z}_{d}

where
\overline{Z}_{d}:=\ker\overline{\partial}_{d}

and
\overline{B}^{\prime}_{d}\simeq\im\overline{\partial}_{d}
is a complementary graded superspace.
By induction,
\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}\overline{B}^{\prime}_{d}=b_{n+k,n}(d)
.
We have that

\dim\overline{C}_{d}=\dim\im\overline{\partial}_{d}+\dim\ker\overline{\partial}_{d}
so, using
lemma

3.3
for the final equality, get that

\displaystyle|c_{n+k,n}(d)|=|b_{n+k,n}(d)|\!+\!\dim\ker\overline{\partial}_{d}\geq|b_{n+k,n}(d)|\!+\!\dim\im\overline{\partial}_{d+1}\geq|b_{n+k,n}(d)|\!+\!|b_{n+k,n}(d+1)|=|c_{n+k,n}(d)|.

This means that equality holds throughout, thereby proving
that
\im\overline{\partial}_{d+1}=\ker\overline{\partial}_{d}
.
The same sequence of equalities without evaluating
at
q=\pi=1
now gives that

\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}\im\overline{\partial}_{d+1}=b_{n+k,k}(d+1)
,
and the argument is complete.

Next we show that

\im\partial_{d}=\ker\partial_{d-1}

and that it is free as a graded right
OH_{n}^{\ell}
-supermodule
of graded superrank
b_{n+k,n}(d)

for each
d=1,\dots,n
. This is a similar induction to the one in the previous paragraph.
For the induction step, we take
0\leq d<n

and assume that we have shown already that

\im\partial_{d}
is free of
graded superrank
b_{n+k,n}(d)
. Consider the short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow Z_{d}\longrightarrow C_{d}\longrightarrow\im\partial_{d}\longrightarrow 0

where
Z_{d}:=\ker\partial_{d}
.
Since
\im\partial_{d}
is free, this short exact sequence splits,
so we have that

C_{d}=B_{d}^{\prime}\oplus Z_{d}

where
B^{\prime}_{d}\simeq\im\partial_{d}
is a complement to
Z_{d}
in
C_{d}

as a graded right
OH_{n}^{\ell}
-supermodule.
Moreover,
\overline{Z}_{d}
from the previous paragraph
is
Z_{d}\otimes 1
. As it is a summand of
C_{d}
, which is free,
we deduce that
Z_{d}
is a free graded right
OH_{n}^{\ell}
-supermodule
with
\operatorname{rk}_{q,\pi}Z_{d}=\operatorname{dim}_{q,\pi}\overline{Z}_{d}=b_{n+k,k}(d+1)
.
The map
\partial_{d+1}:C_{d+1}\rightarrow Z_{d}

is surjective because
\id\otimes\partial_{d+1}:\overline{C}_{d+1}\rightarrow\overline{Z}_{d}
is surjective according to the previous paragraph.
We deduce that
\im\partial_{d+1}=\ker\partial_{d}

is free of graded superrank
b_{n+k,k}(d+1)
, and the argument
is complete.

So now we have shown that
\im\partial_{d}=\ker\partial_{d-1}

is free as a graded right
OH_{n}^{\ell}
-supermodule
of graded superrank
b_{n+k,n}(d)
for
d=1,\dots,n
.
The same is true as a graded left
OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}
-supermodule
since
OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}\cong OH_{n}^{\ell}
and all of the numerology is the same.

To complete the proof of
theorem

12.3
, it just remains
to prove the assertion about the top degree homology.
As
OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}\cong OH_{n}^{\ell}
, it suffices to show that it is
free
of graded superrank

(\pi q^{2})^{\binom{n+1}{2}+nk}
both as a graded right
OH_{n}^{\ell}
- and as a graded left
OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}
-supermodule.
We have already shown that the image of
\partial_{n}
is free
of graded superrank
b_{n+k,k}(n)
. Hence, since
C_{n}
is free of
graded superrank
c_{n+k,n}(n)
by
lemma

12.6
,
we deduce that
\ker\partial_{n}

is free of graded superrank
c_{n+k,n}(n)-b_{n+k,k}(n)
.
Thus, we are reduced to showing that

c_{n+k,n}(n)-b_{n+k,k}(n)=(\pi q^{2})^{\binom{n+1}{2}+nk}.

This follows from the following calculation:

\displaystyle c_{n+k,n}(n)-b_{n+k,k}(n)

\displaystyle=(\pi q^{-2})^{\binom{n}{2}}q^{(n+k)n}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{2n+k}{n}_{q,\pi}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-(\pi q^{-2})^{\binom{n}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}(\pi q^{2})^{(n+k)(n-s-1)}q^{(n+k-1)(s+1)}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{n+k+s}{s+1}_{q,\pi}

\displaystyle=(\pi q^{-2})^{\binom{n}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{n}(\pi q^{2})^{(n+k)(n-s)}q^{(n+k-1)s}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{n+k+s-1}{s}_{q,\pi}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-(\pi q^{-2})^{\binom{n}{2}}\sum_{s=1}^{n}(\pi q^{2})^{(n+k)(n-s)}q^{(n+k-1)s}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{n+k+s-1}{s}_{q,\pi}

\displaystyle=(\pi q^{2})^{(n+k)n-\binom{n}{2}}=(\pi q^{2})^{\binom{n+1}{2}+nk}.

Here, we have used the definitions in
lemma

3.3
for the first equality and
corollary

3.4
for the second.
∎

Remark 12.8
.

From
theorem

12.1
(1)–(2),
tensoring with the
d
th term

U^{\ell}_{(k+d);n-d}\otimes_{OH_{n-d}^{\ell}}V_{n-d;(d)}^{\ell}

in the singular Rouquier complex
corresponds at the level of Grothendieck groups to the

\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]^{\pi}
-module homomorphism

1_{-k}\mathbf{V}(-\ell)\rightarrow 1_{k}\mathbf{V}(-\ell)

defined by the action of

q^{(n-d)(k+d)+d(\ell-3(n-d)-2d+1)}E^{(k+d)}F^{(d)}1_{2n-\ell}=q^{nk}\left(q^{d}E^{(k+d)}F^{(d)}1_{2n-\ell}\right).

From the original definition of the map
T
in

theorem

3.6
,
it follows that multiplication by the
Euler characteristic of
eq.

12.5

corresponds to

q^{nk}T:1_{-k}\mathbf{V}(-\ell)\rightarrow 1_{k}\mathbf{V}(-\ell)
.
Given that the homology of the complex
vanishes in all but the top degree,
it then follows by
eq.

12.2
that the
top homology is

(\pi q^{2})^{\binom{n+1}{2}+nk}\left[OH_{n^{\prime}}^{\ell}\right]

which, reassuringly, agrees with the final assertion of
theorem

12.3
.

Proof of
theorem

12.3
.

Fix
d
with
1\leq d\leq n
.
Define an
initial pair
to be

(\lambda,\mu)\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(k+d)\times n}}\times{\Lambda^{\!+}_{d\times(n-d)}}

such that

\lambda_{k+d}=d-1-s
and
\mu_{d-s}=n-d

for some
0\leq s\leq d-1
. This condition is illustrated by the following picture:

Let
I
be the set of all initial pairs. Note that

(12.8)

|I|=|b_{n+k,n}(d)|.

To see this, the number of
(\lambda,\mu)\in I

with
\lambda_{k+d}=d-1-s
and
\mu_{d-s}=n-d

is
|{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(k+d-1)\times(n-d+s+1)}}\times{\Lambda^{\!+}_{s\times(n-d)}}|
, which is

\binom{n+k+s}{n-d+s+1}\binom{n-d+s}{s}
.
Summing
6
6

6

This is all we need here, but with a little more care using also
corollary

3.2
,
this argument can be used to show that
the coefficient of
q^{2r}\pi^{r}
in
b_{n+k,n}(d)

is equal to
the number of
(\lambda,\mu)\in I
with
|\lambda|+|\mu|=2r
, explaining the
definition of
b_{n+k,n}(d)
itself rather than merely
its evaluation at
q=\pi=1
.
over
s=0,1,\dots,d-1

gives
|b_{n+k,n}(d)|
by the original definition of this natural number.

Also define a
terminal pair

to be

(\kappa,\nu)\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(k+d-1)\times n}}\times{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(d-1)\times(n-d+1)}}

such that

\kappa_{k+d-1}\geq d-1-s
and
\nu_{d-s}<\nu_{d-s-1}=n-d+1

for some
0\leq s\leq d-1
:

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Let
T
be the set of all terminal pairs.
Our final combinatorial observation
is that there is a bijection

(12.9)

f:I\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits}}T

taking

(\lambda,\mu)\in I
to
(\lambda^{-},\mu^{+})\in T

where
\lambda^{-}
is obtained from
\lambda
by removing the
bottom row of its Young diagram, and

\mu^{+}
is obtained from
\mu
by
adding one box to the end of the first
(d-s)
rows
then removing completely the top row.

Now we are going to make an explicit computation of
the differential

\bar{\partial}_{d}
in terms of the basis
for
\overline{C}_{d}=C_{d}\otimes_{OH_{n}^{\ell}}\mathbb{F}

consisting of the vectors

(12.10)

w(\lambda,\mu):=u_{n-d;(k+d)}\big(\bar{\sigma}^{(k+d)}_{\lambda}\big)\otimes v_{n-d;(d)}\big(\bar{\sigma}^{(d)}_{\mu}\big)\otimes 1\in U_{(k+d);n-d}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n-d}^{\ell}}V_{n-d;(d)}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n}^{\ell}}\mathbb{F}

for
(\lambda,\mu)\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(k+d)\times n}}\times{\Lambda^{\!+}_{d\times(n-d)}}
; cf.
lemma

12.6
.
Order pairs
(\kappa,\nu)\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(k+d-1)\times n}}\times{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(d-1)\times(n-d+1)}}

so that
(\kappa^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})<(\kappa,\nu)

if either
|\kappa^{\prime}|<|\kappa|
,
or
|\kappa^{\prime}|=|\kappa|
and
\nu^{\prime}<_{{\operatorname{lex}}}\nu
.
We claim for
(\lambda,\mu)\in I
that

(12.11)

\overline{\partial}_{d}(w(\lambda,\mu))=\pm w(\lambda^{-},\mu^{+})+\text{
(a linear combination of $w(\kappa,\nu)$ for $(\kappa,\nu)<(\lambda^{-},\mu^{+})$)}.

Given the claim, it follows by

eqs.

12.8
and
12.9
that

|\im\overline{\partial}_{d}|\geq|b_{n+k,n}(d)|
, so that the theorem
follows by
lemma

12.7
.

It remains to prove
eq.

12.11
.
Take
(\lambda,\mu)\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(k+d)\times n}}\times{\Lambda^{\!+}_{d\times(n-d)}}

and consider
\overline{\partial}_{d}(w(\lambda,\mu))
.
According to
the definition of
\overline{\partial}_{d}
, we have to apply three
different maps to
w(\lambda,\mu)
arising from
b_{(d+k-1),(1)}
,

c_{(1),(d-1)}
and
\operatorname{ev}_{n-d}
. We apply these maps one by one.

•

First, the map
b_{(d+k-1),(1)}
comes from the embedding

OS\!ym_{k+d}\hookrightarrow OS\!ym_{(k+d-1,1)}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits}}OS\!ym_{k+d-1}\otimes\mathbb{F}[x].

lemma

7.7
shows that this embedding takes

s_{\lambda}^{(k+d)}

to
\sum_{\kappa}\pm s_{\kappa}^{(k+d-1)}\otimes x^{|\lambda|-|\kappa|}

summing over all

\kappa\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(k+d-1)\times n}}
whose Young diagram is
obtained by removing boxes from the bottoms of different columns of
the Young diagram of
\lambda
,
necessarily including all

\lambda_{k+d}

boxes on its
(k+d)
th row.
We say simply “
\kappa
obtained by removing a row strip from
\lambda
”
for this from now on.

•

Next, we apply the map
c_{(1),(d-1)}
, which comes from the embedding

OS\!ym_{d}\hookrightarrow OS\!ym_{(1,d-1)}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits}}\mathbb{F}[x]\otimes OS\!ym_{d-1},

plus some extra signs due to the parity shift.
The version of Pieri obtained by applying
\gamma_{d}

to
lemma

7.7
(using also
eq.

6.8
)
shows that this
takes
\sigma_{\mu}^{(d)}

to
\sum_{\delta}\pm x^{|\mu|-|\delta|}\otimes\sigma_{\delta}^{(d-1)}

summing over

\delta\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(d-1)\times(n-d)}}
whose Young diagram is obtained by removing
boxes from the bottoms of different columns of the Young diagram
of
\mu
, necessarily including all
\mu_{d}
boxes on its
d
th row,
to obtain the Young diagram of partition
\delta
.
We say simply “
\delta
obtained by removing a row strip from
\mu
” for this from now on.

•

So far, remembering
eq.

10.6
,
we have shown that

\big(b_{(k+d-1),(1)}\otimes\;c_{(1),(d-1)}\otimes\id\big)\circ(\operatorname{inc}\otimes\id)

takes
w(\lambda,\mu)

to

\sum_{(\kappa,\delta)}\pm u_{(k+d-1);n-d+1}\big(\sigma_{\kappa}^{(k+d-1)}\big)\otimes u_{n-d}\big(x^{|\lambda|-|\kappa|}\big)\otimes v_{n-d}\big(x^{|\mu|-|\delta|}\big)\otimes v_{n-d+1;(d-1)}\big(\sigma_{\delta}^{(d-1)}\big)\otimes 1

summing over
(\kappa,\delta)\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(k+d-1)\times n}}\times{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(d-1)\times(n-d)}}

such that
\kappa
is obtained by removing a row strip from

\lambda
and
\delta
is obtained by removing a row strip from
\mu
.
Then we use the definition in

theorem

11.3
to
apply
(\operatorname{can}\otimes\id)\circ(\id\otimes\operatorname{ev}_{n-d}\otimes\id\otimes\id)
, giving

\overline{\partial}_{d}(w(\lambda,\mu))=\sum_{(\kappa,\delta)}\pm u_{(k+d-1);n-d+1}\big(\sigma_{\kappa}^{(k+d-1)}\big)\otimes\bar{\eta}^{(n-d+1)}_{(|\lambda|-|\kappa|)+(|\mu|-|\delta|)-(n-d)}v_{n-d+1;(d-1)}\big(\sigma_{\delta}^{(d-1)}\big)\otimes 1.

summing over all
(\kappa,\delta)\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(k+d-1)\times n}}\times{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(d-1)\times(n-d)}}

obtained by removing a row strip from
(\lambda,\mu)\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(k+d)\times n}}\times{\Lambda^{\!+}_{d\times(n-d)}}
.

It remains to commute the
elements
\bar{\eta}^{(n-d+1)}_{(|\lambda|-|\kappa|)+(|\mu|-|\delta|)-(n-d)}
to the right hand side in this expression.
In view of
lemma

10.4
(1)
and degree considerations, this will produce
some linear combination of basis vectors of the form
w(\kappa,\nu)

for
\nu\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(d-1)\times(n-d+1)}}
with
|\nu|=|\mu|+(|\lambda|-|\kappa|)-(n-d)
.
We just need to show that
w(\lambda^{-},\mu^{+})
appears with
coefficient
\pm 1
and all other
w(\kappa,\nu)

that arise satisfy
(\kappa,\nu)<(\lambda^{-},\mu^{+})
.
This is clearly the case if
|\kappa|<|\lambda^{-}|
,
so we may assume from now on that
\kappa
, like
\lambda^{-}
,
is obtained from
\lambda

by removing the minimal number of boxes, i.e., just
its bottom row. So we have that
\kappa=\lambda^{-}

and
|\lambda|-|\kappa|=\lambda_{k+d}
, which equals
d-s-1
for
a unique
0\leq s<d
.
Also let
p:=(d-s+1)+(|\mu|-|\delta|)-(n-d)
for short and consider

\bar{\eta}^{(n-d+1)}_{p}v_{n-d+1;(d-1)}\big(\sigma_{\delta}^{(d-1)}\big)\otimes 1.

By
theorem

6.3
, we have that

\eta^{(n-d+1)}_{p}=s^{(n-d+1)}_{(p)}

plus a linear combination of other
s^{(n-d+1)}_{\tau}

for partitions
\tau
with
|\tau|=p
and
{\operatorname{ht}}(\tau)>1
.
Also
\sigma_{(1^{p})}^{(d-1)}=\varepsilon_{p}^{(d-1)}
.
Using
eq.

10.13
, we deduce that

(12.12)

\bar{\eta}^{(n-d+1)}_{p}v_{n-d+1;(d-1)}\big(\sigma_{\delta}^{(d-1)}\big)\otimes 1=\pm v_{n-d+1;(d-1)}\big(\sigma_{\delta}^{(d-1)}\varepsilon_{p}^{(d-1)}\big)\otimes 1+(*)

where
(*)

is a linear combination of
terms of the form

v_{n-d+1;(d-1)}\big(\sigma_{\delta}^{(d-1)}\sigma^{(d-1)}_{\tau}\big)\otimes 1

for partitions
\tau
with
|\tau|=p
and
\tau_{1}>1
.
We can compute all of these products of dual Schur polynomials
by conjugating with
{\gamma}_{d-1}
and
using the odd Littlewood-Richardson rule; see
remark

7.8
.
Remembering also that

v_{n-d+1;(d-1)}\big(\sigma_{\nu}^{(d-1)}\big)\otimes 1=0

unless
\nu\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(d-1)\times(n-d+1)}}
by
lemma

10.4
(1) again,
we obtain a linear combination of
basis vectors

v_{n-d+1;(d-1)}\big(\sigma_{\nu}^{(d-1)}\big)\otimes 1

for
\nu\in{\Lambda^{\!+}_{(d-1)\times(n-d+1)}}
obtained from
\delta

by adding
p
boxes in particular ways.
If
p<d-s-1
then we cannot have
\nu=\mu^{+}

since that has
d-s+1
boxes in the rightmost column,
whereas that column is empty in
\delta
.
Now suppose that
p=d-s-1
; then
\delta
is
\mu
with
all
(n-d)
boxes in its first row removed.
In this case,
the leading term

\pm v_{n-d+1;(d-1)}\Big(\sigma_{\delta}^{(d-1)}\varepsilon_{p}^{(d-1)}\big)\otimes 1
computed via the odd Littlewood-Richardson rule
does produce
\pm v_{n-d+1;(d-1)}\big(\sigma_{\mu^{+}}^{(d-1)}\big)\otimes 1

when a column strip of
p
boxes is added at the top right of the Young diagram of
\delta
. All other basis vectors coming from this leading term
are of the form

v_{n-d+1;(d-1)}\big(\sigma_{\nu}^{(d-1)}\big)\otimes 1

for
\nu<_{{\operatorname{lex}}}\mu^{+}
.
The basis vectors coming from the lower terms

v_{n-d+1;(d-1)}\big(\sigma_{\delta}^{(d-1)}\sigma^{(d-1)}_{\tau}\big)\otimes 1

for
\tau
with
|\tau|=p
and
\tau_{1}>1

must also all be of the form

v_{n-d+1;(d-1)}\big(s_{\nu}^{(d-1)}\big)\otimes 1

for
\nu<_{{\operatorname{lex}}}\mu^{+}
since when
\tau_{1}>1
the odd Littlewood-Richardson rule
does not allow all
p
boxes to be added to the same column of the
Young diagram of
\delta
.
∎

13.
Non-degeneracy of the odd 2-category

\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})

In this section, we will use the
string calculus for strict graded monoidal supercategories and 2-supercategories
adopting all of the conventions from
[
BE1
]
. In particular,

f\circ g
is vertical composition (
f
on top of
g
) and
f\otimes g
or simply
fg
is horizontal composition
(
f
to the left of
g
).
The following definition originated in
[
EL
]
and was reformulated in the present terms in
[
BE2
]
.

Definition 13.1
.

The
odd
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
2-category

is the strict graded
2
-supercategory

\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
with object set
\mathbb{Z}
,
generating 1-morphisms

E1_{k}=1_{k+2}E:k\rightarrow k\!+\!2
and

1_{k}F=F1_{k+2}:k\!+\!2\rightarrow k
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}

whose identity 2-morphisms are represented graphically
by
\Big\uparrow\>{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\scriptstyle k}={\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\scriptstyle k+2}\>\Big\uparrow
and

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\scriptstyle k}\>\Big\downarrow=\Big\downarrow\>{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\scriptstyle k+2}
, respectively,
and generating 2-morphisms

(13.1)

\displaystyle:EF1_{k}\oplus 1_{k}^{\oplus(-k)}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits}}FE1_{k}

for
k\leq 0
.

The morphisms depicted in
eqs.

13.5
and
13.6

represent a
(k+1)\times 1
matrix
and a
1\times(1-k)
matrix of 2-morphisms
in
\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
, respectively, i.e., they are 2-morphisms in the additive envelope of
\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
. Saying that they are isomorphisms means that
there are some
further generating
2
-morphisms in
\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
which provide
the matrix entries of two-sided inverses to these morphisms.

The defining relations
eqs.

13.1
,
13.2
,
13.3
,
13.5
and
13.6
look quite innocent but they imply many further relations.
In order to record some of these, we first
need to introduce some further shorthand for
generating
2
-morphisms whose existence is provided by the inversion relation. First, we have the leftward crossing and the leftward cups and caps

(13.7)

Following Lauda’s convention from
[
L1
,
L2
]
,
we introduce new shorthands for
endomorphisms of
1_{k}
called “fake bubbles”:
we have clockwise bubbles
decorated by
r+k-1
dots on their left boundary for all
r\leq-k

which denote
(-1)^{(k+1)r}\beta_{k}(\dot{\varepsilon}_{r})
if
r\geq 0
or
0
if
r<0
,
and we have counterclockwise bubbles decorated by
r-k-1

dots on their right boundary for all
r\leq k
which denote

(-1)^{(k+1)r}\beta_{k}(\dot{\eta}_{r})
if
r\geq 0
or
0
if
r<0
.

•

Centrality of the odd bubble
; cf.
[
BE2
, (7.15)]
.
The “odd bubble”

\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\scriptstyle{k}
is shorthand for

{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}\!\!\!\circ
\scriptstyle{k}
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\scriptstyle{k}

if
k\geq 0
or

\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\scriptstyle{k}
{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}\!\!\!\circ
\scriptstyle{-k}

if
k\leq 0
.
So it is

(-1)^{k+1}\beta_{k}(\dot{o})\in\End_{\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})}(1_{k})
.
These are odd 2-morphisms whose square is zero.
Moreover, they are strictly central:

(13.13)

\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\scriptstyle{k}

The following theorem is an odd analog of
[
L2
, Th. 4.12]
.
Our proof is shorter since we are using the more efficient
presentation of
definition

13.1
(although afterwards there is still work to do to determine the
images of the leftward cups and caps).

Theorem 13.2
.

Fix
\ell\geq 0
.
There is a graded 2-superfunctor

\Psi_{\ell}:\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})\rightarrow\mathpzc{OGBim}_{\ell}

with the following properties.

(1)

On objects,

\Psi_{\ell}
takes

2n-\ell

to the graded superalgebra

OH_{n}^{\ell}
for
0\leq n\leq\ell
.
All other objects of
\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
go to the trivial graded superalgebra.

(2)

On generating 1-morphisms,

\Psi_{\ell}
takes

E1_{2n-\ell}
to
the graded superbimodule

Q^{-n}U_{n}^{\ell}

and
1_{2n-\ell}F
to
the graded superbimodule
Q^{3n-\ell+1}V_{n}^{\ell}
, respectively,
both for
0\leq n<\ell
.
All other generating 1-morphisms necessarily go to trivial graded superbimodules.

(3)

On generating 2-morphisms,

\Psi_{\ell}
takes

{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}\!\!\!\circ
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\scriptstyle{2n-\ell}

\displaystyle\mapsto\Big(\rho_{(1);n}(x_{1}):Q^{-n}U_{n}^{\ell}\rightarrow Q^{-n}U_{n}^{\ell}\Big)

\displaystyle(0\leq n\leq\ell-1)

\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\scriptstyle{2n-\ell}

\displaystyle\mapsto\Big(-\rho_{(1^{2});n}(\tau_{1}):Q^{-2n-1}U_{n+1}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n+1}^{\ell}}U_{n}^{\ell}\rightarrow Q^{-2n-1}U_{n+1}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n+1}^{\ell}}U_{n}^{\ell}\Big)

\displaystyle(0\leq n\leq\ell-2)

\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\scriptstyle{2n-\ell}

\displaystyle\mapsto\Big(\operatorname{coev}_{n}:OH_{n}^{\ell}\rightarrow Q^{2n-\ell+1}V_{n}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n+1}^{\ell}}U_{n}^{\ell}\Big)

\displaystyle(0\leq n\leq\ell-1)

\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\scriptstyle{2n-\ell+2}

\displaystyle\mapsto\Big(\operatorname{ev}_{n}:Q^{2n-\ell+1}U_{n}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n}^{\ell}}V_{n}^{\ell}\rightarrow OH_{n+1}^{\ell}\Big)

\displaystyle(0\leq n\leq\ell-1)

Here,
\rho_{(1^{d});n}(a)
is the superbimodule endomorphism
from
eq.

11.21
,
and
\operatorname{ev}_{n}
and
\operatorname{coev}_{n}
are as in
theorem

11.3
9
9

9

We
mean the same underlying linear maps—in some places we have
applied some degree shifts (but no parity shifts) so that they are
not now being viewed as homomorphisms between exactly the same
graded
superbimodules as before.
.
All other generating 2-morphisms are taken to zero.

Proof.

Note to start with that the assignments in (3) are superbimodule
homomorphisms of the correct degrees and parities; cf.
eq.

13.9
.
Viewing
\mathpzc{OGBim}_{\ell}
as a strict graded 2-supercategory
as explained in
remark

10.7
, we will simply construct

\Psi_{\ell}
as a strict
graded 2-superfunctor by checking that the defining relations from

definition

13.1
are all satisfied.
There are three sets of relations,

eq.

13.2
,
eq.

13.3
and
eq.

13.5
–
eq.

13.6
.

The right adjunction relations
eq.

13.3
follow immediately from
theorem

11.3
.

Let us check the odd nil-Hecke relations
from
eq.

13.2
. The
formulation of these relations in
eq.

13.2

differs by signs from the formulation in
eqs.

5.1
,
5.4
,
5.2
,
5.5
,
5.3
and
5.6
.
This discrepancy is explained by the signs in formula
eq.

10.18
.
To be clear about this,
for
a\in ONH_{d}
,
let
\rho_{(1^{d});n}(a)
be the

\big(OH_{n+d}^{\ell},OH_{n}^{\ell}\big)
-superbimodule
endomorphism from
eq.

11.21
viewed now as an endomorphism
of the degree-shifted
Q^{-nd-\binom{d}{2}}U^{\ell}_{n+d-1}\otimes_{OH_{n+d-1}^{\ell}}\cdots\>\>\otimes_{OH_{n+1}^{\ell}}U^{\ell}_{n}
,
for
0\leq d\leq n
and
0\leq n\leq\ell-d
.
The definition from (3) implies more generally that

(13.22)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\ell}\left(\hbox to73.14pt{\vbox to26.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 28.17673pt\lower-17.14468pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{i-1}\rho_{(1^{d});n}(x_{i}),

(13.23)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\ell}\left(\hbox to79.97pt{\vbox to27.02pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 35.00548pt\lower-17.71065pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-14.43088pt}{-13.01656pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{20.76923pt}{-13.63669pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-31.67247pt}{-13.81168pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{26.65785pt}{-0.15419pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)

\displaystyle=-(-1)^{j-1}\rho_{(1^{d});n}(\tau_{j}).

We check
eq.

13.23
, leaving the easier
eq.

13.22
to the reader.
We must show that

\id\otimes\cdots\otimes\rho_{(1^{2});n+j-1}(\tau_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\id=(-1)^{j-1}\rho_{(1^{d});n}(\tau_{j}).

We do this by checking that both sides take the same value on

u_{n+d-1}\big(x^{\kappa_{d}}\big)\otimes\cdots\otimes u_{n}\big(x^{\kappa_{1}}\big)

for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
.
Let
\sum_{\kappa^{\prime}}
denote summation over

\kappa^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
with
\kappa_{i}^{\prime}=\kappa_{i}
for
i\neq j,j+1
.
Suppose that

x_{j+1}^{\kappa_{j+1}}x_{j}^{\kappa_{j}}\cdot\tau_{j}=\sum_{\kappa^{\prime}}c_{\kappa^{\prime}}x_{j+1}^{\kappa_{j+1}^{\prime}}x_{j}^{\kappa_{j}^{\prime}}
.
Then, using
eq.

10.18
, the left hand side gives

\sum_{\kappa^{\prime}}(-1)^{1+(\kappa_{j}-\kappa^{\prime}_{j})+\kappa_{j}+\cdots+\kappa_{d}}c_{\kappa^{\prime}}u_{n+d-1}\big(x^{\kappa_{d}^{\prime}}\big)\otimes\cdots\otimes u_{n}(x^{\kappa_{1}^{\prime}}\big)

and the right hand side gives

(-1)^{j-1}\sum_{\kappa^{\prime}}(-1)^{d-1+(d-j)(\kappa_{j}-\kappa_{j}^{\prime})+(d-j-1)(\kappa_{j+1}-\kappa_{j+1}^{\prime})+\kappa_{j}+\cdots+\kappa_{d}}c_{\kappa^{\prime}}u_{n+d-1}\big(x^{\kappa^{\prime}_{d}}\big)\otimes\cdots\otimes u_{n}\big(x^{\kappa^{\prime}_{1}}\big).

These are equal because

\kappa_{j}-\kappa_{j}^{\prime}+\kappa_{j+1}-\kappa_{j+1}^{\prime}=1

whenever
c_{\kappa^{\prime}}\neq 0
.

With
eqs.

13.22
and
13.23
in hand, the relations
eq.

13.2
are easily checked.
For example, to check the length three braid relation,
we must show that

\rho_{(1^{3});n}(\tau_{2})\circ\big(-\rho_{(1^{3});n}(\tau_{1})\big)\circ\rho_{(1^{3});n}(\tau_{2})=\big(-\rho_{(1^{3});n}(\tau_{1})\big)\circ\rho_{(1^{3});n}(\tau_{2})\circ\big(-\rho_{(1^{3});n}(\tau_{1})\big).

Translating to
U_{(1^{3});n}^{\ell}

using the isomorphism
b_{(1)^{3}}
, the
left hand side becomes the map
u_{(1^{3});n}(f)\mapsto(-1)^{\operatorname{par}(f)}u_{(1^{3});n}(f\cdot\tau_{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2})

and the right hand side becomes

u_{(1^{3});n}(f)\mapsto-(-1)^{\operatorname{par}(f)}u_{(1^{3});n}(f\cdot\tau_{1}\tau_{2}\tau_{1})
.
These are equal due to the sign in the
relation
eq.

5.5
.
To check the third relation in
eq.

13.2
,
we must show that

\rho_{(1^{2});n}(x_{1})\circ\big(-\rho_{(1^{2});n}(\tau_{1})\big)+\rho_{(1^{2});n}(\tau_{1})\circ\rho_{(1^{2});n}(x_{2})=\rho_{(1^{2});n}(\id).

The left hand side corresponds to the map

u_{(1^{2});n}(f)\mapsto u_{(1^{2});n}(f\cdot\tau_{1}x_{1}-f\cdot x_{2}\tau_{1})
,
which equals
u_{(1^{2});n}(f)
by
eq.

5.6
.

Next we check
eqs.

13.5
and
13.6
.
There is nothing to do if
\ell=0
(the zero map is an isomorphism between zero superbimodules!) so assume that
\ell>0
.
Take a weight
k=2n-\ell
of
\mathbf{V}(-\ell)

for some
0\leq n\leq\ell
.
Setting
n^{\prime}:=\ell-n-1
, we have that
k=n-n^{\prime}-1
.
For brevity, we will ignore grading shifts since they play no role in this place, i.e., we work with ordinary rather than
graded superbimodules.

We first check
eq.

13.5
, so
k\geq 0
or, equivalently,

n\geq n^{\prime}+1
.
We need to show that the superbimodule homomorphism
f

defined by the
(n-n^{\prime})\times 1
matrix

\left(\>\sigma_{n}\quad\operatorname{ev}_{n-1}\quad\cdots\quad\operatorname{ev}_{n-1}\circ\big(\rho_{(1);n-1}(x)^{n-n^{\prime}-2}\otimes\id\big)\right)^{T}\!:U_{n-1}\otimes_{OH_{n-1}^{\ell}}\!V_{n-1}^{\ell}\rightarrow V_{n}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n+1}^{\ell}}U_{n}^{\ell}\oplus(OH_{n}^{\ell})^{\oplus(n-n^{\prime}-1)}

is an isomorphism where
\sigma_{n}
, the image of the rightward crossing,
is the superbimodule homomorphism described explicitly in
lemma

11.9
,
or the zero map in the extremal case
n=\ell,n^{\prime}=-1
.
By
lemma

10.8
,
the domain of
f
is free as a right
OH_{n}^{\ell}
-supermodule
with basis

\big\{u_{n-1}(x^{r})\otimes v_{n-1}(x^{s})\>\big|\>0\leq r\leq n^{\prime}+1,0\leq s\leq n-1\big\}
,
and the codomain of
f
is
free as a right
OH_{n}^{\ell}
-supermodule with basis

\big\{v_{n}(x^{s})\otimes u_{n}(x^{r})\>\big|\>0\leq r\leq n^{\prime},0\leq s\leq n\big\}\cup\big\{b_{1},\dots,b_{n-n^{\prime}-1}\big\}

where
b_{i}
is the identity element in the
i
th copy of
OH_{n}^{\ell}
.
Both of these sets are of size
nn^{\prime}+2n
, so it suffices
to show that
f
is
surjective
.
To prove this, since
OH_{n}^{\ell}
is graded local,
it is enough to show that the homomorphism

\bar{f}:=f\otimes 1

obtained by applying the functor
-\otimes_{OH_{n}^{\ell}}\mathbb{F}

is surjective.
Let
uv(r,s)
denote

u_{n-1}(x^{r})\otimes v_{n-1}(x^{s})\otimes 1
and

vu(s,r)
denote
v_{n}(x^{s})\otimes u_{n}(x^{r})\otimes 1
.
Thus, the domain of
\bar{f}
has linear basis

\{uv(r,s)\>|\>0\leq r\leq n^{\prime}+1,0\leq s\leq n-1\}

and the codomain has linear basis

\{vu(s,r)\>|\>0\leq r\leq n^{\prime},0\leq s\leq n\}\cup\{b_{1}\otimes 1,\dots,b_{n-n^{\prime}-1}\otimes 1\}
.
By
eq.

11.23
and
theorem

11.3
, we have that

(13.24)

\bar{f}\big(uv(r,s)\big)=\pm vu(n,r+s-n)\pm b_{n-r-s}\otimes 1\pm vu(s,r)

for
0\leq r\leq n^{\prime}+1
and
0\leq s\leq n-1
,
where the first term should be interpreted as zero if
r+s-n<0

and the middle term should be interpreted as zero
if
n-r-s<1
or
n-r-s>n-n^{\prime}-1
.
Note also that the last term
vu(s,r)
is zero for
r>n^{\prime}
by degree considerations.
The argument is completed with the following observations.

•

We get the vectors

vu(n,r)
for
0\leq r\leq n^{\prime}
from the images of
the basis vectors
uv(n^{\prime}+1,s)
for
n-n^{\prime}-1\leq s\leq n-1
.
Indeed, in the formula
eq.

13.24

for
\bar{f}(uv(n^{\prime}+1,s))
for these values of
s
,
the second and third terms on the right hand side are both zero.

•

Modulo the span of vectors already obtained,
we get the vectors
b_{1}\otimes 1,\dots,b_{n-n^{\prime}-1}\otimes 1

from the images of the basis vectors

uv(n^{\prime}+1,s)
for
0\leq s\leq n-n^{\prime}-2
.
Indeed, in the formula for
f(uv(n^{\prime}+1,s))
for these values of
s

the third term on the right hand side is zero.

•

Modulo the span of vectors already obtained,
we get the vectors

vu(s,r)

for
0\leq r\leq n^{\prime}
and
0\leq s\leq n-1

from the images of the remaining basis vectors
uv(r,s)

for these values of
r
and
s
.

Now consider
eq.

13.6
, so
k\leq 0
and
n^{\prime}\geq n-1
.
We need to show that the superbimodule homomorphism
f

defined by the
1\times(n^{\prime}-n+2)
matrix

\left(\>\sigma_{n}\quad\operatorname{coev}_{n}\quad\cdots\quad\big(\id\otimes\rho_{(1);n}(x)^{n^{\prime}-n}\big)\circ\operatorname{coev}_{n}\right):U_{n-1}\otimes_{OH_{n-1}^{\ell}}\!V_{n-1}^{\ell}\oplus(OH_{n}^{\ell})^{\oplus(n^{\prime}-n+1)}\rightarrow V_{n}^{\ell}\otimes_{OH_{n+1}^{\ell}}U_{n}^{\ell}

is an isomorphism,
where
\sigma_{n}
is as in
lemma

11.9
or the zero map
in the extremal case
n=0,n^{\prime}=\ell-1
.
By
lemma

10.8
,
the domain of
f
is free as a right
OH_{n}^{\ell}
-supermodule
with basis
\big\{u_{n-1}(x^{r})\otimes v_{n-1}(x^{s})\>\big|\>0\leq r\leq n^{\prime}+1,0\leq s\leq n-1\big\}\cup\big\{b_{1},\dots,b_{n^{\prime}-n+1}\big\}
,
where
b_{i}
is the identity element in the
i
th copy of
OH_{n}^{\ell}
,
and the codomain of
f
is
free as a right
OH_{n}^{\ell}
-supermodule with basis

\big\{v_{n}(x^{s})\otimes u_{n}(x^{r})\>\big|\>0\leq r\leq n^{\prime},0\leq s\leq n\big\}
.
Both of these sets are of size
nn^{\prime}+n+n^{\prime}+1
, so it suffices to
see that
f
is surjective. Again, we apply
-\otimes_{OH_{n}^{\ell}}\mathbb{F}

and show that the resulting map
\bar{f}:=f\otimes 1
is surjective.
Let
uv(r,s):=u_{n-1}(x^{r})\otimes v_{n-1}(x^{s})\otimes 1

and
vu(s,r):=v_{n}(x^{s})\otimes u_{n}(x^{r})\otimes 1
for short.
So the domain of
\bar{f}
has linear basis

\{uv(r,s)\>|\>0\leq r\leq n^{\prime}+1,0\leq s\leq n-1\}\cup\{b_{1}\otimes 1,\dots,b_{n^{\prime}-n+1}\otimes 1\}

and the codomain has linear basis

\{vu(s,r)\>|\>0\leq r\leq n^{\prime},0\leq s\leq n\}
.
By
eq.

11.23
and
eq.

11.15
, we have that

(13.25)

\displaystyle\bar{f}\big(uv(r,s)\big)

\displaystyle=\pm vu(n,r+s-n)\pm vu(s,r),

\displaystyle\bar{f}(b_{i}\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=vu(n,i-1),

for
0\leq r\leq n^{\prime}+1
,
0\leq s\leq n-1

and
1\leq i\leq n^{\prime}-n+1
,
interpreting
vu(r+s-n)
as zero if
r+s-n<0

and
s
.
The proof is completed by the following.

•

We get
vu(n,r)
for
0\leq r\leq n^{\prime}-n
from the
images of the vectors
b_{i}\otimes 1
for
i=1,\dots,n^{\prime}-n+1
.

•

We get
vu(n,r)
for
n^{\prime}-n+1\leq r\leq n^{\prime}
from the images of the
vectors
uv(n^{\prime}+1,r)
for
0\leq r\leq n-1
. This uses the observation
that
vu(r,n^{\prime}+1)=0
.

•

Modulo the span of vectors already obtained, we get the remaining
vu(s,r)
for
0\leq s\leq n-1

and
0\leq r\leq n^{\prime}
from the images of the vectors

uv(r,s)
for the same values of
r
and
s
.

∎

from
theorem

13.2
, that is, the superbimodule homomorphisms

.

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{r}(-1)^{(n^{\prime}+1)r+(n+1)s+\binom{s}{2}}\Big[\big(\psi_{n+1}^{\ell}\big)^{-1}\big(\bar{\varepsilon}_{r-s}^{(n^{\prime})}\big)\Big]\bar{\eta}_{s}^{(n+1)}

,

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-30.33138pt}{1.65459pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.80934pt}{2.5266pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)(1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{r}(-1)^{(n^{\prime}+s)r+(n+1)s+\binom{s}{2}}\Big[\big(\psi_{n}^{\ell}\big)^{-1}\big(\bar{\eta}_{r-s}^{(n^{\prime}+1)}\big)\Big]\bar{\varepsilon}_{s}^{(n)}

if
r\geq n-n^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Recalling
eq.

10.2
, the map
p_{n^{\prime}}
is of degree
n-2n^{\prime}
and parity
n^{\prime}\pmod{2}
,
and
q_{n}
is of degree
n-\ell+1
and parity
n\pmod{2}
.
The inverse of the map
p_{n^{\prime}}^{-1}\otimes q_{n}

is
(-1)^{nn^{\prime}}p_{n^{\prime}}\otimes q_{n}^{-1}
.
With this in mind,
we let

\displaystyle\widehat{\operatorname{coev}}_{n}

\displaystyle:=(-1)^{\binom{n}{2}+n^{\prime}}\big(p_{n^{\prime}}\otimes q_{n}^{-1}\big)\circ\operatorname{coev}_{n}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\widehat{\operatorname{ev}}_{n}

\displaystyle:=(-1)^{\binom{n+1}{2}+n^{\prime}}\operatorname{ev}_{n}^{\prime}\circ\big(q_{n}\otimes p_{n^{\prime}}^{-1}\big).

These are both even of degree 0.
To prove the first part of the lemma, we must show that

\widehat{\operatorname{coev}}_{n}=\widetilde{\operatorname{coev}}_{n}

and
\widehat{\operatorname{ev}}_{n}=\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{n}
.

We first show that

\widehat{\operatorname{coev}}_{n}
and
\widehat{\operatorname{ev}}_{n}

are the counit and unit of an adjunction.
This follows from the left adjunction relations
eq.

13.11
:

\displaystyle\big(\id\otimes\widehat{\operatorname{ev}}_{n}\big)\circ\big(\widehat{\operatorname{coev}}_{n}\otimes\id\big)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\binom{n}{2}+\binom{n+1}{2}}(\id\otimes\operatorname{ev}_{n}^{\prime})\circ\big(\id\otimes q_{n}\otimes p_{n^{\prime}}^{-1}\big)\circ\big(p_{n^{\prime}}\otimes q_{n}^{-1}\otimes\id\big)\circ(\operatorname{coev}_{n}^{\prime}\otimes\id)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+n^{\prime}}(\id\otimes\operatorname{ev}_{n}^{\prime})\circ\big(p_{n^{\prime}}\otimes\id\otimes\id\big)\circ\big(\id\otimes\id\otimes p_{n^{\prime}}^{-1}\big)\circ(\operatorname{coev}_{n}^{\prime}\otimes\id)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell-1}p_{n^{\prime}}\circ(\id\otimes\operatorname{ev}_{n}^{\prime})\circ(\operatorname{coev}_{n}^{\prime}\otimes\id)\circ p_{n^{\prime}}^{-1}=\id,

\displaystyle\big(\widehat{\operatorname{ev}}_{n}\otimes\id\big)\circ\big(\id\otimes\widehat{\operatorname{coev}}_{n}\big)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\binom{n}{2}+\binom{n+1}{2}}(\operatorname{ev}_{n}^{\prime}\otimes\id)\circ\big(q_{n}\otimes p_{n^{\prime}}^{-1}\otimes\id\big)\circ\big(\id\otimes p_{n^{\prime}}\otimes q_{n}^{-1}\big)\circ(\id\otimes\operatorname{coev}_{n}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{ev}_{n}^{\prime}\otimes\id)\circ\big(\id\otimes\id\otimes q_{n}^{-1}\big)\circ\big(q_{n}\otimes\id\otimes\id\big)\circ(\id\otimes\operatorname{coev}_{n}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=q_{n}^{-1}\circ(\operatorname{ev}_{n}^{\prime}\otimes\id)\circ(\id\otimes\operatorname{coev}_{n}^{\prime})\circ q_{n}=\id.

Here, for brevity, we are implicitly assuming that
\mathpzc{OGBim}_{\ell}

is strict as in
remark

10.7
.

So now we have two adjunctions
making
(\widetilde{V}^{\ell}_{n},\widetilde{U}_{n}^{\ell})

into a dual pair, one
A_{1}
with unit
\widehat{\operatorname{ev}}_{n}
and counit

\widehat{\operatorname{coev}}_{n}
just constructed,
and the other
A_{2}
with unit
\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{n}
and counit

\widetilde{\operatorname{coev}}_{n}
coming from
theorem

11.5
.
Any such adjunction
A
induces a degree 0 even

\big(OH_{n}^{\ell},OH_{n+1}^{\ell}\big)
-superbimodule
isomorphism

\alpha:\widetilde{U}^{\ell}_{n}\mathrel{{\mathop{\rightarrow}\limits}}\Hom_{OH_{n}^{\ell}{\text{-}}}(\widetilde{V}_{n}^{\ell},OH_{n}^{\ell})
.
So from
A_{1}
and
A_{2}

we get isomorphisms
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
,
hence, an even degree 0 automorphism

\alpha_{2}^{-1}\circ\alpha_{1}
of
\widetilde{U}^{\ell}_{n}
.
By
lemma

10.8
(2c),
\widetilde{U}^{\ell}_{n}
is cyclic generated by the vector

{\tilde{u}}_{n}(1)
. Moreover, this vector spans the (one-dimensional) graded component of
\widetilde{U}^{\ell}_{n}
of lowest degree.
So we must have that

\alpha_{2}^{-1}\circ\alpha_{1}=c_{n}\id

for
c_{n}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
.

The argument in the previous paragraph
shows that

\widehat{\operatorname{coev}}_{n}=c_{n}\;\widetilde{\operatorname{coev}}_{n}

and
\widehat{\operatorname{ev}}_{n}=c_{n}^{-1}\;\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{n}

for some
c_{n}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
.

Given this, taking
r=0
in one of these equations and using that the
“bottom bubbles”

\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\scriptstyle{n-n^{\prime}+1}
{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}\!\!\!\circ
\scriptstyle{n-n^{\prime}}

and

{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}\!\!\!\circ
\scriptstyle{n^{\prime}-n}

are identities if
n\geq n^{\prime}
or
n\leq n^{\prime}
, respectively,
gives that
c_{n}=1
, and the lemma follows.

In this paragraph, we prove
eq.

13.30
.
We need to apply

\operatorname{ev}_{n}\circ\big(\rho_{(1);n}(x)^{n-n^{\prime}+r}\otimes\id\big)\circ\operatorname{coev}_{n}^{\prime}
to
1\in OH_{n+1}^{\ell}

using that
\operatorname{coev}_{n}^{\prime}=(-1)^{\binom{n}{2}+(n+1)n^{\prime}}c_{n}\;\big(p_{n^{\prime}}^{-1}\otimes q_{n}\big)\circ\widetilde{\operatorname{coev}}_{n}
.
Applying
\widetilde{\operatorname{coev}}_{n}
to 1 using the second formula for that in
theorem

11.5
gives

\sum_{s=0}^{n^{\prime}}(-1)^{\ell s+\binom{s}{2}}\Big[\big(\psi_{n+1}^{\ell}\big)^{-1}\big(\bar{\varepsilon}_{n^{\prime}-s}^{(n^{\prime})}\big)\Big]{\tilde{u}}_{n}(1)\otimes{\tilde{v}}_{n}(x^{s}).

Then we scale by

(-1)^{\binom{n}{2}+(n+1)n^{\prime}}c_{n}
and apply

(p_{n^{\prime}}^{-1}\otimes q_{n})
to get

c_{n}\sum_{s=0}^{n^{\prime}}(-1)^{\binom{n}{2}+(n+1)n^{\prime}+\ell s+\binom{s}{2}+ns+n^{\prime}(n^{\prime}-s)}\Big[\big(\psi_{n+1}^{\ell}\big)^{-1}\big(\bar{\varepsilon}_{n^{\prime}-s}^{(n^{\prime})}\big)\Big]u_{n}(1)\otimes v_{n}(x^{s}).

This is
\operatorname{coev}^{\prime}_{n}(1)
.
Then we apply
\rho_{(1);n}(x)^{n-n^{\prime}+r}\otimes\id

(the dots on the left boundary of the bubble)
using
eq.

11.21

to get

c_{n}\sum_{s=0}^{n^{\prime}}(-1)^{\binom{n}{2}+(n+1)n^{\prime}+\ell s+\binom{s}{2}+ns+n^{\prime}(n^{\prime}-s)+(n-n^{\prime}+r)(n^{\prime}-s)+\binom{n-n^{\prime}+r}{2}}\Big[\big(\psi_{n+1}^{\ell}\big)^{-1}\big(\bar{\varepsilon}_{n^{\prime}-s}^{(n^{\prime})}\big)\Big]{\tilde{u}}_{n}(x^{n-n^{\prime}+r})\otimes{\tilde{v}}_{n}(x^{s}).

Finally we apply
\operatorname{ev}_{n}
using the formula from
theorem

11.3

to obtain

c_{n}\sum_{s=n^{\prime}-r}^{n^{\prime}}(-1)^{\binom{n}{2}+(n+1)n^{\prime}+\ell s+\binom{s}{2}+ns+n^{\prime}(n^{\prime}-s)+(n-n^{\prime}+r)(n^{\prime}-s)+\binom{n-n^{\prime}+r}{2}}\Big[\big(\psi_{n+1}^{\ell}\big)^{-1}\big(\bar{\varepsilon}_{n^{\prime}-s}^{(n^{\prime})}\big)\Big]\bar{\eta}^{(n+1)}_{r+s-n^{\prime}}.

It remains to reindex the summation replacing
s
by
s+(n^{\prime}-r)

and to simplify the signs to obtain
eq.

13.30
.

{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.52 -8.9 L 2.52 12.6}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-1.0}{1.0}{0.0}{1.82101pt}{-6.62874pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.90123pt}{-1.08405pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-26.91681pt}{-5.17415pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\Big)=\lambda_{n;(1)}(x_{1})

by the definition
eq.

13.8
,
theorem

13.2
and
lemma

11.8
.
Now we calculate by applying

(-1)^{\binom{n^{\prime}-n+r}{2}}\operatorname{ev}^{\prime}_{n}\circ\big(\lambda_{n;(1)}(x)^{n^{\prime}-n+r}\otimes\id\big)\circ\operatorname{coev}_{n}
to
1\in OH_{n}^{\ell}

using that
\operatorname{ev}_{n}^{\prime}=(-1)^{\binom{n+1}{2}+(n+1)n^{\prime}}c_{n}^{-1}\;\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{n}\circ\big(q_{n}^{-1}\otimes p_{n^{\prime}}\big)
.
By
eq.

11.15
, we have that

\operatorname{coev}_{n}(1)=\sum_{s=0}^{n}v_{n}\big(x^{s}\big)\otimes u_{n}(1)\bar{\varepsilon}^{(n)}_{n-s}.

Then we scale by

(-1)^{\binom{n^{\prime}-n+r}{2}+\binom{n+1}{2}+(n+1)n^{\prime}}c_{n}^{-1}
and apply
\lambda_{n;(1)}(x)^{n^{\prime}-n+r}\otimes\id
to obtain

c_{n}^{-1}\sum_{s=0}^{n}(-1)^{\binom{n^{\prime}-n+r}{2}+\binom{n+1}{2}+(n+1)n^{\prime}}v_{n}\big(x^{n^{\prime}-n+r+s}\big)\otimes u_{n}(1)\bar{\varepsilon}^{(n)}_{n-s}.

Next
q_{n}^{-1}\otimes p_{n^{\prime}}
gives

c_{n}^{-1}\sum_{s=0}^{n}(-1)^{\binom{n^{\prime}-n+r}{2}+\binom{n+1}{2}+(n+1)n^{\prime}+n^{\prime}(n^{\prime}+r+s)}{\tilde{v}}_{n}\big(x^{n^{\prime}-n+r+s}\big)\otimes{\tilde{u}}_{n}(1)\bar{\varepsilon}^{(n)}_{n-s}.

Finally we apply
\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{n}
using the formula in

theorem

11.5
to obtain

c_{n}^{-1}\sum_{s=n-r}^{n}(-1)^{\binom{n^{\prime}-n+r}{2}+\binom{n+1}{2}+(n+1)n^{\prime}+n^{\prime}(n^{\prime}+r+s)+n(n^{\prime}-n+r+s)+\binom{n^{\prime}-n+r+s}{2}}\Big[\big(\psi_{n}^{\ell}\big)^{-1}\Big(\bar{\eta}_{r+s-n}^{(n^{\prime}+1)}\Big)\Big]\bar{\varepsilon}^{(n)}_{n-s}.

It remains to replace
s
by
n-s
and simplify the sign to obtain
eq.

13.31
.
∎

The formulae for the positively
dotted bubbles in
eqs.

13.28
and
13.29

are rather complicated.
To simplify, one can apply the homomorphism
\alpha_{n}^{\ell}

from
lemma

8.3
, to obtain the following.

Corollary 13.4
.

For
\ell=n+n^{\prime}
and
k=n-n^{\prime}
,
the graded superalgebra homomorphism defined by the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\End_{\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})}(1_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\End_{OH_{n}{\text{-}}OH_{n}}(OH_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces OH_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{n^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta\mapsto\theta(1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{n}^{\ell}}

is equal to the canonical quotient map
R\twoheadrightarrow R_{n^{\prime}},\dot{c}\mapsto\dot{c}^{(n^{\prime})}
.

Proof.

We note first that this composition is indeed a graded superalgebra homomorphism. Now we use
eqs.

13.28
and
13.29
to show
for all
r\geq 1
that it takes

\dot{\varepsilon}_{r}\mapsto\dot{\varepsilon}_{r}^{(n^{\prime})}

in the case
k\geq 0

and
\dot{\eta}_{r}\mapsto\dot{\eta}_{r}^{(n^{\prime})}

in the case
k\leq 0
.
The arguments are similar in the two cases, so we just give
the details for
k\geq 0
, i.e.,
n\geq n^{\prime}
.
If
\ell=0
the result is trivial, so we may assume
\ell>0
,
hence,
n\geq 1
.
Remembering
the definition of
\beta_{k}(\dot{\varepsilon}_{r})
from
eq.

13.12
,
we apply
eq.

13.28
with
n
replaced by
n-1
to get that

\Psi_{\ell}(\beta_{k}(\dot{\varepsilon}_{r}))(1)=(-1)^{(n-n^{\prime}+1)r}\sum_{s=0}^{r}(-1)^{(n^{\prime}+1)r+ns+\binom{s}{2}}\Big[\big(\psi_{n}^{\ell}\big)^{-1}\big(\bar{\varepsilon}_{r-s}^{(n^{\prime})}\big)\Big]\bar{\eta}_{s}^{(n)}.

From
eq.

8.6
, it follows that
\alpha_{n}^{\ell}\Big(\big(\psi_{n}^{\ell}\big)^{-1}\big(\bar{e}_{r}^{(n^{\prime})}\big)\Big)=(-1)^{nr}\dot{e}_{r}^{(n^{\prime})}
, hence,

\alpha_{n}^{\ell}\Big(\big(\psi_{n}^{\ell}\big)^{-1}\big(\bar{\varepsilon}_{r}^{(n^{\prime})}\big)\Big)=(-1)^{nr}\dot{\varepsilon}_{r}^{(n^{\prime})}
.
So

\alpha_{n}^{\ell}\big(\Psi_{\ell}(\beta_{k}(\varepsilon_{r}))(1)\big)=(-1)^{(n-n^{\prime}+1)r+(n^{\prime}+1)r+nr}\dot{\varepsilon}_{r}^{(n^{\prime})}=\dot{\varepsilon}_{r}^{(n^{\prime})}
.
∎

The results so far in this section have an application to prove the

non-degeneracy
of
\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
, which was conjectured in
[
EL
,
BE2
]
.
This asserts that the 2-morphism spaces in
\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
have the expected
graded dimensions. The result may be formulated as follows.
For any
k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}

and 1-morphisms
X,Y\in\Hom_{\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})}(k,\ell)
(i.e., words
consisting of
m
letters
E
and
n
letters
F

such that
\ell=k+2m-2n
)
we view the 2-morphism
space

\Hom_{\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})}(X,Y)
as a graded right
R
-supermodule
so that
\dot{c}\in R

acts by horizontally composing on the right with
\beta_{k}(\dot{c})
.

Theorem 13.5
.

For
k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}

and

X,Y\in\Hom_{\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})}(k,\ell)
,
the 2-morphism space

\Hom_{\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})}(X,Y)

is free as a graded right
R
-supermodule
with basis given by a set of representatives for
equivalence classes of decorated
reduced
(X,Y)
-matchings
in the sense defined in
[
BE2
, Sec.8]
.
In particular,
\beta_{k}:R\rightarrow\End_{\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})}(1_{k})

is an isomorphism for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

The “easy” step in the proof is to show that

\Hom_{\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})}(X,Y)
is spanned as a right
R
-supermodule
by the 2-morphisms that are the
representatives for
equivalence classes of decorated
reduced
(X,Y)
-matchings. This is proved by exhibiting
an explicit straightening algorithm
going by induction on the number of crossings. See
[
BE2
, Th. 8.1]
, which simply cites
[
KL
, Prop. 3.11]
as
the argument is the same as in the purely even setting, or
[
DEL
]
for a more systematic treatment.
Note the straightening algorithm requires all of the relations described above, including the alternating braid relation.

The “hard” step is to establish the linear independence.
By a standard reduction, which is again the same as in the ordinary even setting
as in
[
KL
, Rem. 3.16]
, it suffices to treat the case that

X=Y=E^{d}
for some
d\geq 0
.
In this case, the
decorated reduced
(X,Y)
-matchings consist of

d
strings oriented from bottom to top decorated with some dots
close to the top boundary.
We index them by pairs
(\kappa,w)
for
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}^{d}

and
w\in S_{d}
.
For such a pair, the corresponding 2-morphism
f(\kappa,w)

has
\kappa_{i}
dots at the top of the
i
th string,
with the strings below arranged so that they represent some reduced expression for

w
.
Consider some linear relation

f:=\sum_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}^{d},w\in S_{d}}f(\kappa,w)\beta_{k}(\dot{c}_{\kappa,w})=0

for
\dot{c}_{\kappa,w}\in R
.
Each
\dot{c}_{\kappa,w}
is an
\mathbb{F}
-linear combination
of basis vectors

\dot{h}_{\lambda}
of
R
for
\lambda
in some finite set
P_{\kappa}

of partitions.
Pick
0\leq n\leq\ell
with

k=2n-\ell
in such a way that

n
and
\ell-n
are both very large
relative to
|\kappa|
and
|\lambda|
for all

\lambda\in P_{\kappa},\kappa\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
with
\dot{c}_{\kappa,w}\neq 0

for some
w\in S_{n}
.
Then we apply the 2-superfunctor
\Psi_{\ell}
to
f
to obtain
the relation

\Psi_{\ell}(f)=\sum_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}^{d},w\in S_{d}}\Psi_{\ell}(f(\kappa,w))\Psi_{\ell}(\beta_{k}(\dot{c}_{\kappa,w}))=0

in
\End_{OH_{n+d}^{\ell}{\text{-}}OH_{n}^{\ell}}\big(U_{n+d-1}\otimes_{OH_{n+d-1}^{\ell}}\cdots\otimes_{OH_{n+1}^{\ell}}U_{n}^{\ell}\big)
.
Conjugating with the isomorphism
b_{(1)^{d};n}

from
eq.

10.18
,
we get from
\Psi_{\ell}(f)
a superbimodule
endomorphism
\tilde{f}=0
of

U_{(1^{d});n}^{\ell}
.
Using
eqs.

13.22
and
13.23
, it follows that

\tilde{f}=\sum_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}^{d},w\in S_{d}}\pm x^{\kappa}\tau_{w}\otimes\Psi_{\ell}(\beta_{k}(\dot{c}_{\kappa,w}))

for some signs, where this is being
viewed as an endomorphism of the free right
OH_{n}^{\ell}
-superbimodule

U_{(1^{d});n}^{\ell}

using the right action of
ONH_{d}
from
lemma

10.5
(2).
By the large choice of
n
and
\ell
,
the endomorphisms defined by each
x^{\kappa}\tau_{w}

are linearly independent;
cf. the proof of
theorem

5.2
.
We deduce that

\Psi_{\ell}\big(\beta_{k}(\dot{c}_{\kappa,w})\big)=0

for all
\kappa
and
w
.

It remains to show that

\Psi_{\ell}\big(\beta_{k}(\dot{c}_{\kappa,w})\big)=0

implies that
\dot{c}_{\kappa,w}=0
for sufficiently large
n
and
\ell
.
Assume that

\Psi_{\ell}\big(\beta_{k}(\dot{c}_{\kappa,w})\big)=0
.
Remembering
that
\dot{c}_{\kappa,w}
is an
\mathbb{F}
-linear combination
of
\dot{h}_{\lambda}
for
\lambda
with
|\lambda|
small,
this follows on evaluating at
1\in OH_{n}^{\ell}
then applying
the homomorphism
\alpha_{n}^{\ell}:OH_{n}^{\ell}\rightarrow R_{\ell-n}
.
The point here is that by

corollary

13.4

we have that

\alpha_{n}^{\ell}\Big(\Psi_{\ell}\big(\beta_{k}(\dot{h}_{\lambda})\big)(1)\Big)=\dot{h}_{\lambda}^{(\ell-n)}.

These elements of
R_{\ell-n}
are linearly independent
for small
\lambda
, so we can conclude that the coefficients
of all
\dot{h}_{\lambda}
in
\dot{c}_{\kappa,w}
are zero.
∎

The following corollary is well known; see also

[
BE2
, Th. 11.7]
for the
explicit definition of the isomorphism.
We just note a different convention for
(q,\pi)
-integers
is used
in
[
BE2
, Sec. 9]
compared to
eq.

3.1
.
This accounts for the difference in the defining
relation
[
BE2
, (9.2)]
for
U_{q,\pi}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})

compared to the relation
eq.

3.11
being used for it here.

Corollary 13.6
.

The split Grothendieck ring
K_{0}\big(\operatorname{g\underline{sKar}}(\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}))\big)

is isomorphic as
a
\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]^{\pi}
-algebra
to the integral form

\mathbf{U}_{q,\pi}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
of
U_{q,\pi}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
defined at the end of
section

3
.
Under the isomorphism,
the isomorphism classes of the 1-morphisms

E1_{k}
and
F1_{k}
correspond to the elements
of
\mathbf{U}_{q,\pi}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
denoted by the same notation.

Proof.

See
[
BE2
, Th. 12.1]
, which explains how to deduce this from the non-degeneracy
given by
theorem

13.5
.
∎

Remark 13.7
.

theorem

13.5
is not new—it was already been established in
[
DEL
]
by a completely different technique.
Also a version of
corollary

13.6
already appeared in
[
EL
]
.
The proof of
theorem

13.5

given here is in the same
spirit as the proof of non-degeneracy of the ordinary
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
2-category given in
[
L1
, Prop. 8.2]
and the more general proof of non-degeneracy for
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
given in
[
KL
]
.

For
d\geq 1
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
there are graded superalgebra homomorphisms

(13.32)

\displaystyle\rho_{d}^{(k)}:ONH_{d}
